Global Results for Weakly Dispersive KP-II Equations on the Cylinder
Authors: Sebastian Herr, Robert Schippa, Nikolay Tzvetkov
Published: 2026

ABSTRACT
We consider the dispersion-generalized KP-II equation on a partially periodic domain in the weakly dispersive regime. We use Fourier decoupling techniques to derive essentially sharp Strichartz estimates. With these in hand, we show that global well-posedness of the quasilinear Cauchy problem in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T). Finally, we prove a long-time decay property of solutions with small mass by using the Kato smoothing effect in the fractional case.

Introduction

We consider the following dispersion-generalized KP-II equation (fKP-II) posed on the cylinder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}= \mathbb {R}/ (2 \pi \mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}T=R/(2πZ):1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \partial _t u - \partial _x D_x^\alpha u + \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y^2 u & = u \partial _x u, \quad (t,x,y) \in \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}, \\ u(0) & = u_0 \in L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}). \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-∂xDxαu+∂x-1∂y2u=u∂xu,(t,x,y)∈R×R×T,u(0)=u0∈L2(R×T).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_x^\alpha $$\end{document}Dxα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _x^{-1}$$\end{document}∂x-1 denote Fourier multipliers, which are defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widehat{(D_x^\alpha f)} (\xi ) = |\xi |^\alpha \hat{f}(\xi ), \; \alpha \in \mathbb {R}, \quad \widehat{(\partial _x^{-1} f)}(\xi ) = (i \xi )^{-1} \hat{f}(\xi ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Dxαf)^(ξ)=|ξ|αf^(ξ),α∈R,(∂x-1f)^(ξ)=(iξ)-1f^(ξ).See [39, Section 5.2.1] for a discussion of the derivation of this model.

We consider real-valued data, which is preserved by the nonlinear evolution. In the present paper we are concerned with global well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-based Sobolev spaces, that is, existence, uniqueness, and continuous dependence of the solution on the initial data and the global behavior of the solution. The Cauchy problem for the KP-II equation is given by (1) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 2$$\end{document}α=2 and is known to admit a smooth data-to-solution mapping in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T) by previous work of the third author with Molinet and Saut [50].

We address the weakly dispersive regime where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha <2$$\end{document}α<2, in which the Cauchy problem is quasilinear, that is, the data-to-solution mapping, if it exists in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-based Sobolev spaces, fails to be smooth (see [48]).

Conserved quantities for real initial data are the mass\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M(u) = \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} u^2 \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M(u)=∫R×Tu2dxdyand the energy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_\alpha (u) = \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} \big ( \frac{1}{2} |D_x^{\frac{\alpha }{2}} u|^2 - \frac{1}{2} |\partial _x^{-1} \partial _y u|^2 + \frac{1}{6} u^3 \big ) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Eα(u)=∫R×T(12|Dxα2u|2-12|∂x-1∂yu|2+16u3)dxdy.Since the energy is indefinite, it can hardly be utilized in a proof of global well-posedness. Here we use the mass, which requires a local well-posedness result in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T).

On \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 we have the scaling symmetry\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u_\lambda (t,x,y) = \lambda ^\alpha u(\lambda ^{\alpha +1} t, \lambda x, \lambda ^{\frac{\alpha }{2}+1} y). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uλ(t,x,y)=λαu(λα+1t,λx,λα2+1y).We define the anisotropic homogeneous Sobolev norm as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_0 \Vert _{\dot{H}^{s_1,s_2}} = \Vert |\xi |^{s_1} |\eta |^{s_2} \hat{u}_0 \Vert _{L^2_{\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u0‖H˙s1,s2=‖|ξ|s1|η|s2u^0‖Lξ,η2.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2 = 0$$\end{document}s2=0, we find that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{H}^{s_1,0}$$\end{document}H˙s1,0 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_1 = 1 - \frac{3 \alpha }{4}$$\end{document}s1=1-3α4 is the scaling-critical space. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s_2 < 0$$\end{document}s2<0 breaks the Galilean invariance, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{H}^{s_1,0}$$\end{document}H˙s1,0 is distinguished.

The KP-II equation was proved to be globally well-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {T}^2)$$\end{document}L2(T2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}L2(R2) by Bourgain [7]. Global well-posedness on the cylinder in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T) was considered by Molinet–Saut–Tzvetkov [50]. We remark that the Kadomtsev–Petviashvili equations were derived to model water waves in two dimensions; see the works by Kadomtsev–Petviashvili [33] and Ablowitz–Segur [1] with an emphasis on modeling transverse perturbations of the KdV soliton. Stability of the line soliton was proved in [47]; see also [51, 52]. For this reason the cylindrical domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T is of importance when studying (1). We remark that on the domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}\times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}T×R the evolution satisfies improved dispersive properties compared to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T; see [17]. On \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 Hadac–Herr–Koch [23, 24] proved scattering in the critical space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\dot{H}^{-\frac{1}{2},0}(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}H˙-12,0(R2) for small initial data.

The Cauchy problem (1) extends the fractional KdV equation on the real line:2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \partial _t u + \partial _x D_x^\alpha u & = u \partial _x u, \quad (t,x) \in \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}, \\ u(0) & = u_0 \in L^2(\mathbb {R}). \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu+∂xDxαu=u∂xu,(t,x)∈R×R,u(0)=u0∈L2(R).For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 2$$\end{document}α=2 the original KdV equation is recovered, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 1$$\end{document}α=1 the model describes the likewise prominent Benjamin–Ono equation. But the nonlinear evolution of these two models is very different in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2(R). The KdV evolution in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2(R) is known to be semilinear with a real analytic data-to-solution mapping as a consequence of the contraction mapping principle. Seminal contributions are due to Bourgain [6] and Kenig–Ponce–Vega [35]. The I-team [12] finally proved sharp semilinear global well-posedness. More recently, Killip–Vişan [37] devised the method of commuting flows, which utilizes the complete integrability pointed out many decades ago by Lax [43] in unweighted Sobolev spaces. They proved the sharp global well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{-1}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}H-1(R).

On the other hand, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < 2$$\end{document}α<2, (2) is a quasilinear Cauchy problem and it is known that it cannot be solved via Picard iteration for any Sobolev regularity [25, 41, 48].

Herr–Ionescu–Kenig–Koch [26] proved global well-posedness of (2) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2(R) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1,2) by using a paradifferential gauge transform. Recently, Ai–Liu [2] reported an improvement to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>\frac{3}{4}(1-\alpha )$$\end{document}s>34(1-α). The gauge transform for the Benjamin–Ono equation was applied for the first time by Tao [58] who showed global well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}H1(R) in combination with Strichartz estimates. Combining the gauge transform with Fourier restriction analysis Ionescu–Kenig [32] showed global well-posedness for the Benjamin–Ono equation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}L2(R) (see also [11, 29, 49]). Recently, Killip et al. [36] proved sharp global well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Hs(R) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>-\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}s>-12 via the method of commuting flows. Coming back to (1), we let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta ) = \xi |\xi |^\alpha - \frac{\eta ^2}{\xi } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ωα(ξ,η)=ξ|ξ|α-η2ξdenote the dispersion relation. We note that for the KP-II equation the resonance function is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Omega _2(\xi _1,\eta _1,\xi _2,\eta _2)&= \omega _2(\xi _1+\xi _2,\eta _1+\eta _2) - \omega _2(\xi _1,\eta _1) - \omega _2(\xi _2,\eta _2) \\&= 3 (\xi _1+\xi _2) \xi _1 \xi _2 + \frac{(\eta _1 \xi _2 - \eta _2 \xi _1)^2}{\xi _1 \xi _2 (\xi _1 + \xi _2)}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ω2(ξ1,η1,ξ2,η2)=ω2(ξ1+ξ2,η1+η2)-ω2(ξ1,η1)-ω2(ξ2,η2)=3(ξ1+ξ2)ξ1ξ2+(η1ξ2-η2ξ1)2ξ1ξ2(ξ1+ξ2).The fact that both terms are of the same sign highlights a nonlinear defocusing effect. This implies that the resonance function for the KP-II equation is at least as large as the resonance function for the KdV equation, for the first time effectively taken advantage of by Bourgain [7] who proved global well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {T}^2)$$\end{document}L2(T2) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^2)$$\end{document}L2(R2).

Low regularity local well-posedness for fKP-II (1) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}R2 was proved by Hadac [22] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 4/3$$\end{document}α>4/3, up to which the problem is semilinear and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-subcritical. Of course, on the cylinder \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T one can apply the energy method to prove a local well-posedness result in isotropic Sobolev spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}Hs(R×T) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s > 2$$\end{document}s>2. We remark that the argument from Linares–Pilod–Saut [44] would yield an improvement of the energy method on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T, but no global result. The main point of this paper is to prove the first global result for the Cauchy problem for fKP-II on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T.

Recently, we [28] extended Bourgain’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-result in the fully periodic case. We obtained local well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s,0}(\mathbb {T}^2)$$\end{document}Hs,0(T2) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>-\frac{1}{90}$$\end{document}s>-190. The proof interpolates short-time bilinear Strichartz estimates and novel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates derived from an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling inequality due to Guth–Maldague–Oh [21].

For (1) we use Fourier restriction analysis on frequency-dependent time intervals and an interpolation argument in a similar way like in [28]. This allows us to show well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T) for (1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < 2$$\end{document}α<2. The decoupling argument recovers the scaling critical Strichartz estimates up to the endpoint provided that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η-frequencies are not too large compared to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ-frequencies. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(\hat{f}) \subseteq \{ (\xi ,\eta ) \in \mathbb {R}^2: |\xi | \sim N, \; |\eta | \lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha }{2}+1} \}.$$\end{document}supp(f^)⊆{(ξ,η)∈R2:|ξ|∼N,|η|≲Nα2+1}. Then we have the following estimate:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert e^{t (\partial _x D_x^{\alpha } - \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y^2)} f \Vert _{L^4_{t}([0,1],L^4_{xy}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}))} \lesssim _\varepsilon N^{\frac{2-\alpha }{8}+ \varepsilon } \Vert f \Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖et(∂xDxα-∂x-1∂y2)f‖Lt4([0,1],Lxy4(R×T))≲εN2-α8+ε‖f‖L2(R×T).In Section 3 we argue in greater generality how the decoupling estimate from [21] yields Strichartz estimates for dispersion relations with uniformly bounded derivatives, which are sharp up to endpoints. The argument does not depend on the domain.

A crucial difference between KP-II on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2$$\end{document}T2 and fKP-II on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T is the occurence of very small frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \ll 1$$\end{document}N≪1. For the fKP-II equations (1) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < 2$$\end{document}α<2, there is clearly no way to show local well-posedness in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s_1,s_2}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}Hs1,s2(R×T) via the contraction mapping principle as this would yield a real analytic well-posedness result for (2) in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s_1}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}Hs1(R). We emphasize that the dispersion-generalized KP-II equations are not known to be completely integrable. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\star :=2-0.015$$\end{document}α⋆:=2-0.015. We show the following:

Theorem 1.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (\alpha ^\star , 2)$$\end{document}α∈(α⋆,2). Then (1) is globally well-posed in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}L2(R×T) for real-valued initial data.

Furthermore, in Proposition 8.1 we show existence of solutions and persistence of regularity in a regularity scale. Since employing a gauge transform for (1) in higher dimensions appears to be very complicated, we opt for the different approach of frequency-dependent time localization. Early instances are due to Koch–Tzvetkov [40], where Strichartz estimates on frequency-dependent time intervals are used to solve the Benjamin–Ono equation. Here we modify the implementation due to Ionescu–Kenig–Tataru [31]. In [31] an analysis on frequency-dependent time intervals was carried out using Fourier restriction norms to solve the KP-I equation globally in the energy space.

Here the frequency-dependent time localization will be chosen depending on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. Moreover, we use a modulation weight to take advantage of large modulations due to the nonlinear defocusing effect. We explain our choice of time localization in Subsection 2.3.

We remark that recently Guo–Molinet [19] obtained unconditional global well-posedness for the KP-I equation in the energy space without using the short-time Fourier restriction spaces (see also [18, 38]). Crucially, they show a priori bounds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_T^p L^{\infty }_{xy}$$\end{document}LTpLxy∞ bounds of the solution which strongly hinges on dispersive effects. The argument to conclude well-posedness still relies on frequency-dependent time localization and a trilinear smoothing estimate related to the nonlinear Loomis–Whitney inequality (cf. [31]). The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 regularity we are dealing with in the present paper seems to be too low to use a similar iteration scheme.

Implementing the interpolation argument in short-time Fourier restriction spaces in the present analyis yields further losses. While the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates are sharp, the interpolation with multilinear estimates can likely be improved to lower the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha ^\star $$\end{document}α⋆ for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 global well-posedness. We make certain choices in the interpolation argument (see Section 5 and Remark 5.3) to simplify the computations and do not strive for optimality here.

With short-time bilinear and energy estimates at hand, the conclusion of the proof of local well-posedness is well-known. We shall be brief in Section 8. More details are given when constructing regular solutions. To this end, we use a Galerkin approximation and a compactness argument to construct solutions.

The starting point to show local well-posedness are a priori estimates for solutions u to (1):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u_i(t) \Vert _{H^{s,0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \lesssim _{t, \Vert u(0) \Vert _{L^2}} \Vert u(0) \Vert _{H^{s,0}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ui(t)‖Hs,0(R×T)≲t,‖u(0)‖L2‖u(0)‖Hs,0.A key point in the proof of the full local well-posedness result is that we estimate differences of solutions at negative Sobolev regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s' = -8(2-\alpha )$$\end{document}s′=-8(2-α). The Lipschitz dependence we show for differences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = u_1-u_2$$\end{document}v=u1-u2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i$$\end{document}ui, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2 solutions to (1), reads\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v(t) \Vert _{H^{s',0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \lesssim _{\Vert u_i(0) \Vert _{L^2}} \Vert v(0) \Vert _{H^{s',0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v(t)‖Hs′,0(R×T)≲‖ui(0)‖L2‖v(0)‖Hs′,0(R×T)for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 \leqq t \leqq T(\Vert u_i(0) \Vert _{L^2})$$\end{document}0≦t≦T(‖ui(0)‖L2). The proof is then concluded by using frequency envelopes which is implemented like in our previous work [28] and based on the general outline detailed in [30]. This argument originates in work of Tao [57].

Further more, we address the long time behavior of small solutions.

Theorem 1.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (\alpha ^*,2)$$\end{document}α∈(α∗,2). There exists \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu >0$$\end{document}μ>0 such that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0$$\end{document}u0 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \Vert u_0\Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})}<\mu $$\end{document}‖u0‖L2(R×T)<μthe solution of (1) established in Theorem 1.1 satisfies for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma > 0$$\end{document}γ>0\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \lim _{t\rightarrow +\infty } \int _{\mathbb {T}}\, \int _{x\geqq \gamma t}u^{2}(t,x,y)\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}y=0. $$\end{document}limt→+∞∫T∫x≧γtu2(t,x,y)dxdy=0.

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 conservation law of (1) implies the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 stability of the zero solution of (1). However, this stability does not give any information on asymptotical stability, that is it does not give any information on how the conserved \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 mass evolves in time. The result of Theorem 1.2 implies that at time t there is essentially no \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 mass in the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x\geqq \gamma t$$\end{document}x≧γt.

It would be very interesting to extend Theorem 1.2 to the case when the zero solution is replaced by a 1d soliton. This would require an approach to the transverse stability of the KP-II line solitons independent of the Miura transform used in [47]. The approach of [46] is less dependent on the integrability of the KP-II equation, and it would be worthwhile to investigate whether a similar approach may lead to the transverse stability of the line solitons of (1).

Moreover, the smallness assumption in Theorem 1.2 cannot be dropped. Indeed, arguing like in the proof of [34, Proposition 1] (see also [14]) one may show that for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>0$$\end{document}c>0 there is a nontrivial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 solution of (1) of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ u(t,x,y)=Q(x-c t), $$\end{document}u(t,x,y)=Q(x-ct),and for such a solution,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \int _{x\geqq \gamma t}\int _{\mathbb {T}}Q^{2}(x-ct)\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}y=2\pi \int _{(\gamma -c) t}^\infty Q^2(x)\textrm{d}x, $$\end{document}∫x≧γt∫TQ2(x-ct)dxdy=2π∫(γ-c)t∞Q2(x)dx,which tends to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2\pi \Vert Q\Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {R})}^2\ne 0$$\end{document}2π‖Q‖L2(R)2≠0 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t\rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}t→∞, provided \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c>\gamma $$\end{document}c>γ. This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu <\sqrt{2\pi } \Vert Q\Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {R})}$$\end{document}μ<2π‖Q‖L2(R) is necessary.

Outline of the paper. In Section 2 we introduce notation and the short-time function spaces. Moreover, we explain our choice of frequency-dependent time localization depending on resonance considerations and short-time Strichartz estimates. In Section 3 we discuss linear Strichartz estimates obtained from decoupling inequalities and in Section 4 we show bilinear Strichartz estimates. In Section 5 we show trilinear convolution estimates, which will interpolate between linear and bilinear Strichartz estimates. In Section 6 we show short-time bilinear estimates and in Section 7 short-time energy estimates, for which we rely on linear and bilinear Strichartz estimates and trilinear convolution estimates. In Section 8 we construct regular solutions and conclude the proof of Theorem 1.1 with short-time bilinear and energy estimates at hand. Finally, in Section 9 we prove Theorem 1.2.

Dyadic numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,L,\ldots \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}N,L,…∈2Z are typically denoted by capital letters.

Dyadic numbers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N,L,\ldots \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}N,L,…∈2Z are typically denoted by capital letters.

Time and space variables are given by (t, x, y), the dual frequency variables are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau ,\xi ,\eta )$$\end{document}(τ,ξ,η).

Time and space variables are given by (t, x, y), the dual frequency variables are denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau ,\xi ,\eta )$$\end{document}(τ,ξ,η).
