Notations and Function Spaces

In the following we introduce the short-time Fourier restriction spaces to carry out the analysis. The notation and conventions closely follow [28, Section 2].

Fourier transform and Littlewood-Paley decomposition

The space-time Fourier transform of a function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}u:R×R×T→C is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} u)(\tau ,\xi ,\eta ) = \hat{u}(\tau ,\xi ,\eta ) = \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} e^{-it \tau } e^{-ix\xi } e^{-iy\eta } u(t,x,y) \textrm{d}t \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Ft,x,yu)(τ,ξ,η)=u^(τ,ξ,η)=∫R×R×Te-itτe-ixξe-iyηu(t,x,y)dtdxdy.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f: \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {Z}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}f:R×R×Z→C, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in L^1_{\tau ,\xi } \ell ^1_{\eta }$$\end{document}f∈Lτ,ξ1ℓη1. The inverse Fourier transform is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}f )(t,x,y) = \frac{1}{(2 \pi )^3} \int _{\mathbb {R}^2} e^{it \tau } e^{ix \xi } \sum _{\eta } e^{iy \eta } f(\tau ,\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d} \tau \textrm{d}\xi . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Ft,x,y-1f)(t,x,y)=1(2π)3∫R2eitτeixξ∑ηeiyηf(τ,ξ,η)dτdξ.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0 \in C^\infty _c(B(0,2))$$\end{document}η0∈Cc∞(B(0,2)) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0 \equiv 1$$\end{document}η0≡1 on [0, 1] and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _0$$\end{document}η0 be radially decreasing. We define\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \eta _1(\tau ) = \eta _0(\tau /2) - \eta _0(\tau ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}η1(τ)=η0(τ/2)-η0(τ).For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}L∈2Z we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _L(\tau ) = \eta _1(\tau /L)$$\end{document}ηL(τ)=η1(τ/L) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _{\leqq L}(\tau )= \eta _0(\tau / L)$$\end{document}η≦L(τ)=η0(τ/L). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}N∈2Z be a dyadic number. We define frequency projections with respect to the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ-frequencies by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_N : L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}) \rightarrow L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}), \quad u \mapsto \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y} (\eta _N(\xi ) \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} u). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN:L2(R×R×T)→L2(R×R×T),u↦Ft,x,y-1(ηN(ξ)Ft,x,yu).

Function spaces

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [1,2]$$\end{document}α∈[1,2]. Recall that the dispersion relation reads\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta ) = \xi |\xi |^\alpha - \frac{\eta ^2}{\xi }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ωα(ξ,η)=ξ|ξ|α-η2ξ.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_0 \in L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}u0∈L2(R×T). We denote the linear propagation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_\alpha (t) u_0 = \frac{1}{(2 \pi )^2} \int _{\mathbb {R}} \sum _{\eta \in \mathbb {Z}} e^{i(x \xi + y \eta + t \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta ))} \hat{u}_0(\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\xi . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Sα(t)u0=1(2π)2∫R∑η∈Zei(xξ+yη+tωα(ξ,η))u^0(ξ,η)dξ.To address the singularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \xi = 0 \}$$\end{document}{ξ=0} of the symbol, we firstly explain the evolution on the dense subspace comprised of functions with frequency support away from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \xi = 0 \}$$\end{document}{ξ=0}. A similar frequency cutoff is carried out in Section 8.1. So we presently omit the details.

We define modulation projections for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0} \cup \{ 0 \}$$\end{document}L∈2N0∪{0} for functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}u∈L2(R×R×T) by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widehat{(Q_L u)} (\tau ,\xi ,\eta ) = \eta _L(\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) \hat{u}(\tau ,\xi ,\eta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(QLu)^(τ,ξ,η)=ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))u^(τ,ξ,η).We introduce a modulation weight already used by Bourgain [7]; see also [28]. This will be crucial to show a suitable estimate in case the high modulation is on the low frequency.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_+ = \max (1,N)$$\end{document}N+=max(1,N). We define function spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z_N \subseteq L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}ZN⊆L2(R×R×Z), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z \in \{X, \bar{X} \}$$\end{document}Z∈{X,X¯} by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Z_N = \{ g \in L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {Z}) \, : \, \text {supp}_{\xi }(g) \subseteq [-4N, -N/2] \cup [N/2, 4N], \; \Vert g \Vert _{Z_N} < \infty \} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ZN={g∈L2(R×R×Z):suppξ(g)⊆[-4N,-N/2]∪[N/2,4N],‖g‖ZN<∞}with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert g \Vert _{X_N}&= \sum _{L \geqq 1} L^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+ L/ N_+^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau -\omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) g \Vert _{L_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }^2}, \\ \Vert g \Vert _{\bar{X}_N}&= \sum _{L \geqq 1} L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau -\omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) g \Vert _{L_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }^2}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖g‖XN=∑L≧1L12(1+L/N+α+1)14‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))g‖Lτ,ξ,η2,‖g‖X¯N=∑L≧1L12‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))g‖Lτ,ξ,η2.Above we indicate the integration in frequency variables in the subindices. Note that the measure is the product of the Lebesgue measure in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ and the counting measure in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta $$\end{document}η.

We let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} D_{N,L}&= \{ (\xi ,\eta ,\tau ) : \; |\xi | \sim N, \, |\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )| \sim L \}, \\ D_{N,\leqq L}&= \{ (\xi ,\eta ,\tau ) : \; |\xi | \sim N, \, |\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )| \leqq L \} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}DN,L={(ξ,η,τ):|ξ|∼N,|τ-ωα(ξ,η)|∼L},DN,≦L={(ξ,η,τ):|ξ|∼N,|τ-ωα(ξ,η)|≦L}denote dyadically localized regions in space-time frequencies with fixed frequency and modulation.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta > 0$$\end{document}β>0 the short-time Fourier restriction norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N$$\end{document}FN at frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}N∈2Z is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{F_N} = \sup _{t_k \in \mathbb {R}} \Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[ \eta _0(N_+^{\beta }(t-t_k)) P_N u] \Vert _{X_N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖FN=suptk∈R‖Ft,x,y[η0(N+β(t-tk))PNu]‖XN.The dual norm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_N$$\end{document}NN prescribed by Duhamel’s formula is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f \Vert _{\mathcal {N}_N} = \sup _{t_k \in \mathbb {R}} \Vert (\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta ) + i N_+^{\beta })^{-1} \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[ \eta _0(N_+^{\beta }(t-t_k)) P_N u] \Vert _{X_N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f‖NN=suptk∈R‖(τ-ωα(ξ,η)+iN+β)-1Ft,x,y[η0(N+β(t-tk))PNu]‖XN.As well \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N$$\end{document}FN as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_N$$\end{document}NN are localized in time by the usual means: Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in (0,1]$$\end{document}T∈(0,1]. For a frequency localized function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u: [-T,T] \times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}u:[-T,T]×R×T→C we let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{G_N(T)} = \inf _{\tilde{u} \big \vert _{[0,T]} = u \big \vert _{[0,T]}} \Vert \tilde{u} \Vert _{G_N}, \quad G \in \{F,\mathcal {N} \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖GN(T)=infu~|[0,T]=u|[0,T]‖u~‖GN,G∈{F,N},where we take the infimum over all extensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{u}: \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}u~:R×R×T→C. We explain in the next subsection why we choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = (2-\alpha ) + \varepsilon $$\end{document}β=(2-α)+ε as time localization for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [1,2]$$\end{document}α∈[1,2].

The following lemma (see [31, Eq. (2.4)]) allows us to localize time to any shorter time intervals than required by the short-time norms provided that we do not use the weight:

Lemma 2.1

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0}$$\end{document}M∈2N0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_N \in \bar{X}_N$$\end{document}fN∈X¯N, the following estimate holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{J \geqq M} J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega (\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} \big | f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) \big | M^{-1} (1+M^{-1} |\tau - \tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d} \tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\quad + M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{\leqq M}(\tau -\omega (\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) | M^{-1} (1 + M^{-1} |\tau - \tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim \Vert f_N \Vert _{\bar{X}_N} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑J≧MJ12‖ηJ(τ-ω(ξ,η))∫R|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2+M12‖η≦M(τ-ω(ξ,η))∫R|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲‖fN‖X¯Nwith implicit constant independent of K and L.

Since we need a variant for the summation with modulation weight, we record the proof.

Proof

For the small modulations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \lesssim M$$\end{document}J≲M we can use Young’s convolution inequality to find that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{\leqq M} (\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) \int |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )| M^{-1} (1+M^{-1} |\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d} \tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\xi ,\eta } L^1_{\tau }} \Vert M^{-1} (1+ M^{-1} |\tau |)^{-4} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau }} \\&\lesssim \sum _{L \geqq 1} L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau -\omega _{\alpha }(\xi ,\eta )) f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau , \xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M12‖η≦M(τ-ωα(ξ,η))∫|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲M12‖fN‖Lξ,η2Lτ1‖M-1(1+M-1|τ|)-4‖Lτ2≲∑L≧1L12‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))fN‖Lτ,ξ,η2.We turn to an estimate of the modulations greater than M. We decompose\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )\\&= \big ( \eta _{\leqq CM}(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) + \sum _{M \ll L \ll J} \eta _L(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) + \sum _{L \sim J} \eta _L(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) + \sum _{L \gg J} \eta _L(\tau '-\omega _\alpha )\big ) \\&\quad \times f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fN(τ′,ξ,η)=(η≦CM(τ′-ωα)+∑M≪L≪JηL(τ′-ωα)+∑L∼JηL(τ′-ωα)+∑L≫JηL(τ′-ωα))×fN(τ′,ξ,η).For the estimate of the first term note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{-1}(1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} \lesssim M^{-1} (J/M)^{-4}$$\end{document}M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4≲M-1(J/M)-4. Then, by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and integrating in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, which incurs a factor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J^{\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}J12, we find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad J^{\frac{1}{2}} M^{-1} (J/M)^{-4} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega _\alpha ) \int \eta _{\leqq C M}(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )| \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim (J/M)^{-3} M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{\leqq C^2 M} f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J12M-1(J/M)-4‖ηJ(τ-ωα)∫η≦CM(τ′-ωα)|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(J/M)-3M12‖η≦C2MfN‖Lτ,ξ,η2.The summation over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \gg M$$\end{document}J≫M is straight-forward.

For the second term we find by Young’s convolution inequality\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{L: M \ll L \ll J} J^{\frac{1}{2}} (J/M)^{-2} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega _\alpha ) \int \eta _{L}(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )| \\&\quad \times M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-2} \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim \sum _{L: M \ll L \ll J} J^{\frac{1}{2}} (J/M)^{-2} L^{\frac{1}{2}} L^{-\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau -\omega _\alpha ) f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \lesssim (J/M)^{-\frac{5}{2}} \Vert f_N \Vert _{\bar{X}_N}, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑L:M≪L≪JJ12(J/M)-2‖ηJ(τ-ωα)∫ηL(τ′-ωα)|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|×M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-2dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲∑L:M≪L≪JJ12(J/M)-2L12L-12‖ηL(τ-ωα)fN‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(J/M)-52‖fN‖X¯N,again with straight-forward summation.

We turn to the third term. In this case we cannot argue by rapid decay of the kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k_M(\tau ,\tau ') = M^{-1}(1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4}$$\end{document}kM(τ,τ′)=M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4, but the almost orthogonality salvages the estimate:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega _\alpha ) \int |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )| \eta _L(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\xi ,\eta } L^2_{\tau }} \\&\lesssim J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert ( \eta _L f_N )* k_M \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \lesssim L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _L f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J12‖ηJ(τ-ωα)∫|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|ηL(τ′-ωα)M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lξ,η2Lτ2≲J12‖(ηLfN)∗kM‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲L12‖ηLfN‖Lτ,ξ,η2.Lastly, for the final term we obtain the estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega _\alpha ) \int |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )| \eta _L(\tau '-\omega _\alpha ) M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim J^{\frac{1}{2}} (L/M)^{-2} L^{-\frac{1}{2}} L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _L f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}J12‖ηJ(τ-ωα)∫|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|ηL(τ′-ωα)M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲J12(L/M)-2L-12L12‖ηLfN‖Lτ,ξ,η2.The summation is again straight-forward. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

The following is immediate by applying:

Corollary 2.2

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}γ∈S(R) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0}$$\end{document}M∈2N0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0 \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}t0∈R, it follows\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[\gamma (M(t-t_0)) \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}(f_N)] \Vert _{\bar{X}_N} \lesssim \Vert f_N \Vert _{\bar{X}_N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ft,x,y[γ(M(t-t0))Ft,x,y-1(fN)]‖X¯N≲‖fN‖X¯N.The implicit constant is independent of M, N, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0 \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}t0∈R.

Proof

We denominate ? that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} [ \gamma (M(t-t_0)) \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}^{-1} (f_N))] \Vert _{\bar{X}_N} \\&\leqq \sum _{J \geqq 1} J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{J}(\tau - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} |f_N|(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ft,x,y[γ(M(t-t0))Ft,x,y-1(fN))]‖X¯N≦∑J≧1J12‖ηJ(τ-ωα(ξ,η))∫RM-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4|fN|(τ′,ξ,η)dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2.We split the sum into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \leqq J \leqq M$$\end{document}1≦J≦M and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \geqq M$$\end{document}J≧M. The first sum is dominated by increasing the support of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta _J$$\end{document}ηJ and carrying out the dyadic sum:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \sum _{1 \leqq J \leqq M} J^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _J(\tau -\omega _{\alpha }(\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} |f_N|(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\leqq M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{\leqq M}(\tau -\omega _{\alpha }(\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} M^{-1} (1+M^{-1}|\tau -\tau '|)^{-4} |f_N|(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑1≦J≦MJ12‖ηJ(τ-ωα(ξ,η))∫RM-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4|fN|(τ′,ξ,η)dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≦M12‖η≦M(τ-ωα(ξ,η))∫RM-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4|fN|(τ′,ξ,η)dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2.Consequently, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[\gamma (M(t-t_0)) \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}(f_N)] \Vert _{\bar{X}_N}$$\end{document}‖Ft,x,y[γ(M(t-t0))Ft,x,y-1(fN)]‖X¯N is dominated by the sum estimated in Lemma 2.1, from which the conclusion follows. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Remark 2.3

To interpret the above, suppose we are estimating an expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma (T'^{-1} (t-t_0)) u_N$$\end{document}γ(T′-1(t-t0))uN which is localized at frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \geqq 1$$\end{document}N≧1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [1,2)$$\end{document}α∈[1,2) and on times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T' \ll T(N)=N^{-(2-\alpha )-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}T′≪T(N)=N-(2-α)-ε. When we do not use the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+L/N^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}}$$\end{document}(1+L/Nα+1)14, we obtain, for the dyadically localized modulation piece,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{L \geqq (T')^{-1}} L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{N,L} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \lesssim \Vert u_N \Vert _{F_N}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑L≧(T′)-1L12‖fN,L‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲‖uN‖FN,where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_{N,L} = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} 1_{D_{N,\leqq T'}} \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} [\gamma (T'^{-1} (t-t_0)) u_N], & \quad L = T', \\ 1_{D_{N,L}} \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} [\gamma (T'^{-1} (t-t_0)) u_N], & \quad L > T'. \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fN,L=1DN,≦T′Ft,x,y[γ(T′-1(t-t0))uN],L=T′,1DN,LFt,x,y[γ(T′-1(t-t0))uN],L>T′.

There are few cases in which we want to take advantage of the low modulation weight. In comparison with the above, we can only dispose the higher time localization if it is not higher than the size of the modulation weight. We have the following variant of Lemma 2.1:

Lemma 2.4

Under the assumptions of Lemma 2.1, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \lesssim N^{\alpha +1} \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0}$$\end{document}M≲Nα+1∈2N0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_N \in X_N$$\end{document}fN∈XN, the following estimate holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\sum _{J \geqq M} J^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+J / N^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert \eta _J(\tau - \omega (\xi ,\eta )) \\&\quad \times \int _{\mathbb {R}} \big | f_N(\tau ,\xi ,\eta ) \big | M^{-1} (1+M^{-1} |\tau - \tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d} \tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\quad + M^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert \eta _{\leqq M}(\tau -\omega (\xi ,\eta )) \int _{\mathbb {R}} |f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta ) | M^{-1} (1 + M^{-1} |\tau - \tau '|)^{-4} \textrm{d}\tau ' \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim \Vert f_N \Vert _{X_N} \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑J≧MJ12(1+J/Nα+1)14‖ηJ(τ-ω(ξ,η))×∫R|fN(τ,ξ,η)|M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2+M12‖η≦M(τ-ω(ξ,η))∫R|fN(τ′,ξ,η)|M-1(1+M-1|τ-τ′|)-4dτ′‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲‖fN‖XNwith implicit constant independent of K and L.

Proof

This is a reprise of the previous proof. The reason the weight becomes admissible for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \lesssim N^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}M≲Nα+1 is that for the imbalanced \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau ,\tau '$$\end{document}τ,τ′ in the decomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_N(\tau ',\xi ,\eta )$$\end{document}fN(τ′,ξ,η) we gain factors \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(J/M)^{-2}$$\end{document}(J/M)-2. By these we can compensate for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$J \gtrsim M$$\end{document}J≳M\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (1+J/N^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} (J/M)^{-\frac{1}{4}} \lesssim (1+M/N^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(1+J/Nα+1)14(J/M)-14≲(1+M/Nα+1)14.The remainder of the argument is like in the proof of Lemma 2.1. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Correspondingly,

Corollary 2.5

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R})$$\end{document}γ∈S(R) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \lesssim N^{\alpha +1} \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0}$$\end{document}M≲Nα+1∈2N0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0 \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}t0∈R, it follows\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[\gamma (M(t-t_0)) \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}(f_N)] \Vert _{X_N} \lesssim \Vert f_N \Vert _{X_N}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ft,x,y[γ(M(t-t0))Ft,x,y-1(fN)]‖XN≲‖fN‖XN.The implicit constant is independent of M, N, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_0 \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}t0∈R.

Remark 2.6

Suppose again we aim to estimate an expression \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma (T'^{-1} (t-t_0) u_N )$$\end{document}γ(T′-1(t-t0)uN) which is localized at frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \gg 1$$\end{document}N≫1 and on times \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T' \ll T(N)=N^{-(2-\alpha )-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}T′≪T(N)=N-(2-α)-ε. When we use the weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(1+L/N^{\alpha +1})^{-1}$$\end{document}(1+L/Nα+1)-1, we can estimate the expressions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{L \geqq (T')^{-1}} L^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+L/N^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert f_{N,L} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \lesssim \Vert u_N \Vert _{F_N}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑L≧(T′)-1L12(1+L/Nα+1)14‖fN,L‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲‖uN‖FN,provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T'\lesssim N^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}T′≲Nα+1.

To justify manipulations for example when carrying out energy estimates, we prefer to work with regular functions. To introduce the suitable regularity scale, we let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(\xi ,\eta ) = 1+|\eta |/|\xi |$$\end{document}p(ξ,η)=1+|η|/|ξ| denote a symbol adapted to the (indefinite) energy. The introduction of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p(\xi ,\eta )$$\end{document}p(ξ,η) will allow us to obtain sufficient regularity in the y-variable by working with the norms3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \phi \Vert _{B^s} = \Vert \langle \xi \rangle ^s p(\xi ,\eta ) \hat{\phi }(\xi ,\eta ) \Vert _{L^2_{\xi ,\eta }(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {Z})}, \quad s \geqq 0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖ϕ‖Bs=‖⟨ξ⟩sp(ξ,η)ϕ^(ξ,η)‖Lξ,η2(R×Z),s≧0for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}ϕ∈L2(R×T). Note that there are \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}ϕ∈S(R×T) for which \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \phi \Vert _{B^s} = \infty $$\end{document}‖ϕ‖Bs=∞, but for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in B^s$$\end{document}f∈Bs we can find \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi _n)_{n \in \mathbb {N}} \subseteq \mathcal {S}$$\end{document}(ϕn)n∈N⊆S with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert \phi _n \Vert _{B^s} < \infty $$\end{document}‖ϕn‖Bs<∞ such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f - \phi _n \Vert _{B^s} \rightarrow 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f-ϕn‖Bs→0.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}s∈R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C([0,T],L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}))$$\end{document}u∈C([0,T],L2(R×T)) we let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert ^2_{F^s(T)} = \sum _{N \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}} N_+^{2s} \Vert P_N u \Vert ^2_{F_N(T)}, \quad \Vert u \Vert ^2_{\mathcal {N}(T)} = \sum _{N \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}} N_+^{2s} \Vert P_N u \Vert ^2_{\mathcal {N}_N(T)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖Fs(T)2=∑N∈2ZN+2s‖PNu‖FN(T)2,‖u‖N(T)2=∑N∈2ZN+2s‖PNu‖NN(T)2.The function spaces are comprised of regular functions in the following sense:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} F^s(T)&= \{ u \in C([-T,T],B^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})) : \Vert u \Vert _{F^s(T)}< \infty ) \}, \\ \mathcal {N}^s(T)&= \{ v \in C([-T,T],B^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})) : \Vert v \Vert _{\mathcal {N}^s(T)} < \infty ) \}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Fs(T)={u∈C([-T,T],B2(R×T)):‖u‖Fs(T)<∞)},Ns(T)={v∈C([-T,T],B2(R×T)):‖v‖Ns(T)<∞)}.

Remark 2.7

The regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^2$$\end{document}B2 is enough to bound the quadratic derivative nonlinearity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 by Sobolev embedding. Consider a strong solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C([0,T],B^2)$$\end{document}u∈C([0,T],B2) to (1), that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u(t) = S_\alpha (t) u_0 + \int _0^t S_{\alpha }(t-s) \partial _x (u^2) \textrm{d}s$$\end{document}u(t)=Sα(t)u0+∫0tSα(t-s)∂x(u2)ds. The following is immediate by applying from Sobolev embedding and the Leibniz rule:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \int _0^t S_\alpha (t-s) \partial _x (u_1(s) u_2(s)) \textrm{d}s \Vert _{L^2_x} \lesssim _T \Vert u_1 \Vert _{C_T B^2} \Vert u_2 \Vert _{C_T B^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∫0tSα(t-s)∂x(u1(s)u2(s))ds‖Lx2≲T‖u1‖CTB2‖u2‖CTB2.

We recall the embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s(T) \hookrightarrow C([-T,T],H^{s,0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}))$$\end{document}Fs(T)↪C([-T,T],Hs,0(R×T)) (cf. [31, Lemma 3.1]):

Lemma 2.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}s∈R, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in (0,1]$$\end{document}T∈(0,1]. Then the following estimate holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{t \in [0,T]} \Vert u(t) \Vert _{H^{s,0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \lesssim \Vert u \Vert _{F^s(T)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supt∈[0,T]‖u(t)‖Hs,0(R×T)≲‖u‖Fs(T).

Frequency-dependent time localization erases the dependence between initial data and the solution at finite time. We define a strengthened version of energy norms to take this into account:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert ^2_{E^s(T)} = \Vert P_{\leqq 8} u(0) \Vert ^2_{L^2} + \sum _{N \geqq 8} N^{2s} \sup _{t \in [0,T]} \Vert P_N u(t) \Vert ^2_{L^2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖Es(T)2=‖P≦8u(0)‖22+∑N≧8N2ssupt∈[0,T]‖PNu(t)‖22.We define the energy space comprised of regular functions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} E^s(T)&= \{ u \in C([-T,T]:B^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})) \, : \, \Vert u \Vert _{E^s(T)} < \infty \}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Es(T)={u∈C([-T,T]:B2(R×T)):‖u‖Es(T)<∞}.The linear energy estimate in short-time Fourier restriction spaces (cf. [31, Proposition 3.2]) reads as follows:

Lemma 2.9

(Duhamel estimate in short-time function spaces) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}s∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in E^s(T) \cap F^s(T)$$\end{document}u∈Es(T)∩Fs(T), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in \mathcal {N}^s(T)$$\end{document}v∈Ns(T) such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _t u - \partial _x D_x^\alpha u + \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y^2 u = v$$\end{document}∂tu-∂xDxαu+∂x-1∂y2u=v. Then the following estimate holds:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{F^s(T)} \lesssim \Vert u \Vert _{E^s(T)} + \Vert v \Vert _{\mathcal {N}^s(T)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖Fs(T)≲‖u‖Es(T)+‖v‖Ns(T).

We shall trade modulation regularity for powers of the time localization. This will be helpful to control the evolution for large data. Define the following variant of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_N$$\end{document}XN-spaces:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f_N \Vert _{X_N^b} = \sum _{L \geqq 1} L^b \Vert \eta _L(\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) f_N \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖fN‖XNb=∑L≧1Lb‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))fN‖Lτ,ξ,η2.Replacing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_N$$\end{document}XN with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_N^b$$\end{document}XNb we obtain short-time function space variants \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N^b$$\end{document}FNb, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_N^b$$\end{document}NNb. These we can localize in time \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N^b(T)$$\end{document}FNb(T), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {N}_N^b(T)$$\end{document}NNb(T) like above. We have the following lemma (cf. [20, Lemma 3.4]):

Lemma 2.10

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in (0,1]$$\end{document}T∈(0,1], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b<\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}b<12. Then it holds that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N u \Vert _{F_N^b(T)} \lesssim T^{\frac{1}{2}-b-} \Vert P_N u \Vert _{F_N(T)} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNu‖FNb(T)≲T12-b-‖PNu‖FN(T)for any function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u:[-T,T] \times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}\rightarrow \mathbb {C}$$\end{document}u:[-T,T]×R×T→C.

Resonance considerations, frequency interactions, and short-time nonlinear and energy estimates

In this section we explain our choice of frequency-dependent time localization by considering specific frequency interactions. Resonance considerations play a crucial role. We give an overview of the short-time estimates for functions localized in modulation and frequency. We consider as Fourier restriction norm on the unit time interval for frequency-localized functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u=(P_{N/2} + P_N + P_{2N}) u$$\end{document}u=(PN/2+PN+P2N)u (roughly corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = 0$$\end{document}β=0 in the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_N(T)$$\end{document}FN(T)):4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{\mathcal {X}_N} = \sum _{L \geqq 1} L^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+L/N_+^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y} [u] \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖XN=∑L≧1L12(1+L/N+α+1)14‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))Ft,x,y[u]‖Lτ,ξ,η2.The dual norm reads as5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert _{\mathcal {X}_N'} = \sup _{L \geqq 1} L^{-\frac{1}{2}} (1+L/N_+^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert \eta _L(\tau - \omega _\alpha (\xi ,\eta )) \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}[u] \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖XN′=supL≧1L-12(1+L/N+α+1)14‖ηL(τ-ωα(ξ,η))Ft,x,y[u]‖Lτ,ξ,η2.If we could show estimates6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N \partial _x (P_{N_1} u P_{N_2} u) \Vert _{\mathcal {X}'_N} \lesssim C(N,N_1,N_2) \Vert P_{N_1} u \Vert _{\mathcal {X}_{N_1}} \Vert P_{N_2} u \Vert _{\mathcal {X}_{N_2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PN∂x(PN1uPN2u)‖XN′≲C(N,N1,N2)‖PN1u‖XN1‖PN2u‖XN2,which are summable in the frequencies, we could apply the contraction mapping principle in Fourier restriction spaces \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}^s$$\end{document}Xs and obtain solutions with analytic dependence on the initial data.

The modulation weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^{\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}L12 allows us to treat functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in \mathcal {X}_N$$\end{document}u∈XN as being sufficiently close to linear solutions to inherit their properties, in particular linear Strichartz estimates. On the other hand, increasing the weight would narrow our resolution space, which distinguishes the modulation regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b=\frac{1}{2}$$\end{document}b=12.

The above estimates are presently further expanded as convolution estimates in frequency-variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\tau ,\xi ,\eta )$$\end{document}(τ,ξ,η)1 for functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i: \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {Z}\rightarrow \mathbb {R}_{\geqq 0}$$\end{document}fi:R×R×Z→R≧0 supported in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{N_i,L_i}$$\end{document}DNi,Li. The key building blocks in the analysis are summable estimates for expressions of the form7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad L^{-\frac{1}{2}} (1+L/N_+^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert 1_{D_{N,L}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim C(N,N_1,N_2) \prod _{i=1}^2 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+L_i/N_{i,+}^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L-12(1+L/N+α+1)14‖1DN,L(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲C(N,N1,N2)∏i=12Li12(1+Li/Ni,+α+1)14‖fi,Ni,Li‖2.But since the data-to-solution mapping fails to be analytic, summable estimates (6) cannot hold true. Here the frequency-dependent time localization comes to rescue, which leads to a minimum localization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L,L_i$$\end{document}L,Li, broadly speaking, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \geqq T(N)^{-1}$$\end{document}L≧T(N)-1, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i \geqq T(N_i)^{-1}$$\end{document}Li≧T(Ni)-1. When summing (7) and establishing the short-time bilinear estimate, we moreover have to take into account the derivative nonlinearity. And in case the input functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i$$\end{document}fi are frequency supported at frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N2 much higher than the output \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \ll N_1$$\end{document}N≪N1, we additionally have to lower the time-localization from T(N) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T(N_1)$$\end{document}T(N1) to estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i$$\end{document}fi with the correct time-localization. This leads to a factor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T(N) / T(N_1)$$\end{document}T(N)/T(N1).

We furthermore obtain trilinear convolution estimates8\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \lesssim C(N,N_1,N_2) \prod _{i=1}^3 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+L_i/N_{i,+}^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3≲C(N,N1,N2)∏i=13Li12(1+Li/Ni,+α+1)14‖fi,Ni,Li‖2,which will play a role as well when proving the short-time bilinear estimates (note that after using duality, (7) essentially becomes (8)) as when proving the short-time energy estimates.

To obtain effective estimates (7) and (8), we require control of the resonance function, which is for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _i, \eta _i \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ξi,ηi∈R, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi _i \ne 0$$\end{document}ξi≠0 given by9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Omega _{\alpha }(\xi _1,\eta _1,\xi _2,\eta _2)&= \omega _\alpha (\xi _1+\xi _2,\eta _1+\eta _2) - \omega _\alpha (\xi _1,\eta _1) - \omega _\alpha (\xi _2,\eta _2) \\&= \underbrace{(\xi _1+\xi _2)|\xi _1+\xi _2|^{\alpha } - \xi _1|\xi _1|^{\alpha } - \xi _2|\xi _2|^{\alpha }}_{\Omega _{\alpha ,1}(\xi _1,\xi _2)} + \underbrace{\frac{(\eta _1 \xi _2 - \eta _2 \xi _1)^2}{\xi _1 \xi _2 (\xi _1+\xi _2)}}_{\Omega _{\alpha ,2}}. \end{aligned}\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ωα(ξ1,η1,ξ2,η2)=ωα(ξ1+ξ2,η1+η2)-ωα(ξ1,η1)-ωα(ξ2,η2)=(ξ1+ξ2)|ξ1+ξ2|α-ξ1|ξ1|α-ξ2|ξ2|α⏟Ωα,1(ξ1,ξ2)+(η1ξ2-η2ξ1)2ξ1ξ2(ξ1+ξ2)⏟Ωα,2.\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{\alpha ,1}$$\end{document}Ωα,1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _{\alpha ,2}$$\end{document}Ωα,2 have the same sign. This generalizes the nonlinear defocusing effect for KP-II, which has already been mentioned in the Introduction. We introduce notations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} X_{\max } = \max ( X, X_1 , X_2), \quad X_{\min } = \min (X, X_1, X_2), \quad X \in \{ N, L \}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Xmax=max(X,X1,X2),Xmin=min(X,X1,X2),X∈{N,L},and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\text {med}} = \max ( \{ L, L_1, L_2 \} \backslash L_{\max } )$$\end{document}Lmed=max({L,L1,L2}\Lmax).

We summarize the key resonance estimate in the following lemma:

Lemma 2.11

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in [1,2]$$\end{document}α∈[1,2], and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi = \xi _1 + \xi _2$$\end{document}ξ=ξ1+ξ2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = \eta _1 + \eta _2$$\end{document}η=η1+η2 for real numbers, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi , \xi _i \ne 0$$\end{document}ξ,ξi≠0, and\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\xi _1 + \xi _2| \sim N, \quad |\xi _1| \sim N_1, \quad |\xi _2| \sim N_2, \quad \{ N, N_1, N_2 \} \subseteq 2^{\mathbb {Z}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ξ1+ξ2|∼N,|ξ1|∼N1,|ξ2|∼N2,{N,N1,N2}⊆2Z.Then we have for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Omega _\alpha $$\end{document}Ωα defined in (9) that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} |\Omega _\alpha (\xi _1,\eta _1,\xi _2,\eta _2)| \geqq |\Omega _{\alpha ,1}(\xi _1,\xi _2)| \gtrsim N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|Ωα(ξ1,η1,ξ2,η2)|≧|Ωα,1(ξ1,ξ2)|≳NmaxαNmin.

The localization present in (7), (8) is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L \sim |\tau - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi ,\eta )|, \; L_1 \sim |\tau _1 - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi _1,\eta _1)|, \; L_2 \sim |\tau _2 - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi _2,\eta _2)| \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L∼|τ-ωα(ξ,η)|,L1∼|τ1-ωα(ξ1,η1)|,L2∼|τ2-ωα(ξ2,η2)|and by convolution constraint, we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\tau - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi _1+\xi _2,\eta _1+\eta _2) - (\tau _1 - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi _1,\eta _1)) - (\tau _2 - \omega _{\alpha }(\xi _2,\eta _2)) \\&\quad = - \Omega _{\alpha }(\xi _1,\eta _1,\xi _2,\eta _2), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τ-ωα(ξ1+ξ2,η1+η2)-(τ1-ωα(ξ1,η1))-(τ2-ωα(ξ2,η2))=-Ωα(ξ1,η1,ξ2,η2),which together with the localization implies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{\max } \gtrsim N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lmax≳NmaxαNmin.When estimating (7) and (8), we distinguish betweenthe resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼NmaxαNmin≫Lmed, in which case the resonance function is as small as it can possibly be,the non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min }$$\end{document}Lmax≫NmaxαNmin, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax≫Lmed,and the strongly non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼Lmed.The strongly non-resonant case is most favorable and can typically be estimated by the simple Strichartz estimate from Lemma 4.2. So, we shall focus on the resonant and non-resonant case. Moreover, we distinguish between High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction, \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb}
