the resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼NmaxαNmin≫Lmed, in which case the resonance function is as small as it can possibly be,

the resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼NmaxαNmin≫Lmed, in which case the resonance function is as small as it can possibly be,

the non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min }$$\end{document}Lmax≫NmaxαNmin, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax≫Lmed,

the non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg N_{\max }^{\alpha } N_{\min }$$\end{document}Lmax≫NmaxαNmin, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax≫Lmed,

and the strongly non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼Lmed.

and the strongly non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim L_{\text {med}}$$\end{document}Lmax∼Lmed.

We explain our choice of time-localization looking into the resonant case in the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction. The derivative loss must be eliminated to propagate the regularity. After applying the space-time Fourier transform and dyadic decomposition in Fourier space, estimates in Fourier restriction norms\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N \partial _x (P_{N_1} u P_{N_2} u) \Vert _{\mathcal {N}_N} \lesssim \Vert P_{N_1} u \Vert _{F_{N_1}} \Vert P_{N_2} u \Vert _{F_{N_2}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PN∂x(PN1uPN2u)‖NN≲‖PN1u‖FN1‖PN2u‖FN2with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \sim N_1 \gg N_2$$\end{document}N∼N1≫N2 follow from\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad L^{-\frac{1}{2}} (1+L/N_+^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} N \Vert 1_{D_{N,L}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim \prod _{i=1}^2 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} (1+L_i/N_{i,+}^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L-12(1+L/N+α+1)14N‖1DN,L(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲∏i=12Li12(1+Li/Ni,+α+1)14‖fi,Ni,Li‖Lτ,ξ,η2.The factor N in the first line reflects the derivative nonlinearity, and we require moreover that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f_{i,N_i,L_i}) \subseteq D_{N_i,L_i}$$\end{document}supp(fi,Ni,Li)⊆DNi,Li. By the resonance considerations, we can suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\max (L_i,L) \gtrsim N_1^\alpha N_2$$\end{document}max(Li,L)≳N1αN2. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \sim N_1^\alpha N_2$$\end{document}L∼N1αN2. By applying Hölder’s inequality and the bilinear Strichartz estimate to be proved in Proposition 4.3, we obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad L^{-\frac{1}{2}} \Vert 1_{D_{N,L}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim (N_1^\alpha N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim \log (N_1) (N_1^\alpha N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} N_2^{\frac{1}{2}} L_{12,\min }^{\frac{1}{2}} \langle L_{12,\max } / N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}} \rangle ^{\frac{1}{2}} \prod _{i=1}^2 \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _{L^2}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L-12‖1DN,L(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(N1αN2)-12‖f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲log(N1)(N1αN2)-12N212L12,min12⟨L12,max/N1α2⟩12∏i=12‖fi,Ni,Li‖L2.This computation shows that in case of small modulation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1 \sim L_2 \sim 1$$\end{document}L1∼L2∼1, the derivative gain recovered from the resonance does not suffice to close the iteration in Fourier restriction norms.

Now we take into account a frequency-dependent time localization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=T(N)=N^{-\beta }$$\end{document}T=T(N)=N-β, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \beta < \alpha $$\end{document}0<β<α. This implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1 \gtrsim N_1^{\beta }$$\end{document}L1≳N1β. Then we can continue to estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad (N_1^\alpha N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} N_2^{\frac{1}{2}} L_{12,\min }^{\frac{1}{2}} \langle L_{12,\max } / N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}} \rangle ^{\frac{1}{2}} \prod _{i=1}^2 \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _{L^2} \\&\lesssim N_1^{-\frac{\alpha }{2}} N_1^{-\frac{\beta }{2}} \prod _{i=1}^2 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _{L^2}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(N1αN2)-12N212L12,min12⟨L12,max/N1α2⟩12∏i=12‖fi,Ni,Li‖L2≲N1-α2N1-β2∏i=12Li12‖fi,Ni,Li‖L2.A frequency-dependent time localization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta = (2-\alpha )+\varepsilon $$\end{document}β=(2-α)+ε will ameliorate the derivative loss and allows us to show short-time nonlinear estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2. The short-time nonlinear estimates are proved in Section 6. The crucial (short-time) bilinear Strichartz estimates are proved in Section 4. The proof of the trilinear convolution estimates (8) is carried out in Section 5. These naturally appear when considering energy estimates of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2-norm of frequency localized solutions. We end the section with a brief explanation of the short-time energy estimates.

The introduction of frequency-dependent time localization leads to the short-time energy estimate in Lemma 2.9 and the necessity to control the short-time energy norm. For sufficiently regular solutions to (1) we obtain by the fundamental theorem of calculus for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert P_N u(t) \Vert _{L^2}^2$$\end{document}‖PNu(t)‖L22:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Vert P_N u(t) \Vert _{L^2}^2&= \Vert P_N u(0) \Vert ^2_{L^2} + \int _0^t \frac{d}{\textrm{d}t} \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} (P_N u)^2(s,x,y) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y \textrm{d}s \\&\quad = \Vert P_N u(0) \Vert ^2_{L^2} + 2 \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N u(s,x,y) \partial _x P_N (u \cdot u)(s,x,y) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNu(t)‖L22=‖PNu(0)‖22+∫0tddt∫R×T(PNu)2(s,x,y)dxdyds=‖PNu(0)‖22+2∫0t∫R×TPNu(s,x,y)∂xPN(u·u)(s,x,y)dxdyds.The different frequency interactions are recovered from a paraproduct decomposition:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_N (u \cdot u) = 2 P_N (u P_{\ll N} u) + P_N (P_{\gtrsim N} u \cdot P_{\gtrsim N} u). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN(u·u)=2PN(uP≪Nu)+PN(P≳Nu·P≳Nu).Note that in the second expression the frequencies must be of comparable modulus by Littlewood-Paley dichotomy and the derivative is already acting on the lowest frequency.

By a standard commutator argument, using the real-valuedness of the solution, this can be accomplished as well for the first term. After expanding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_{\ll N} u = \sum _{K \ll N } P_K u$$\end{document}P≪Nu=∑K≪NPKu we morally have to estimate expressions\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N u P_N u (\partial _x P_K u) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}y \textrm{d}s \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0t∫R×TPNuPNu(∂xPKu)dxdydsfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K \ll N$$\end{document}K≪N. To estimate this in terms of the short-time Fourier restriction norm, we have to subdivide [0, t] into intervals of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=T(N)=N^{-\beta }$$\end{document}T=T(N)=N-β. This incurs a loss of size \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sim |t| N^{\beta }$$\end{document}∼|t|Nβ. This indicates that for the proof of favorable energy estimates we should choose the frequency-dependent time localization as large as possible to require a sub-division of [0, t] into intervals as few as possible and distinguishes the value found above \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=T(N)=N^{-(2-\alpha +\varepsilon )}$$\end{document}T=T(N)=N-(2-α+ε).

Going further, after (smoothly) localizing time to an interval of length \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-\beta }$$\end{document}N-β, we find expressions of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} (\gamma (N^{\beta }(t-t_k)) P_N u) (\gamma (N^{\beta }(t-t_k)) P_N u) (\partial _x P_K u). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R×R×T(γ(Nβ(t-tk))PNu)(γ(Nβ(t-tk))PNu)(∂xPKu).Note that we have some leeway in the time localization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_K u$$\end{document}PKu. We can again choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T=T(N)=N^{-\beta }$$\end{document}T=T(N)=N-β, but can also enlarge the time localization. Then, after applying the space-time Fourier transform, we see that it suffices to estimate an expression\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} N_2 \sum _{L_i,L} \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N2∑Li,L∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3.with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2 \sim K$$\end{document}N1∼N3≫N2∼K. Compared to the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction encountered in the short-time nonlinear interaction, we had to add the time localization, which incurs the loss \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1^{\beta }$$\end{document}N1β, but for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \ll N_1^{1-\beta }$$\end{document}N2≪N11-β the commutator argument produces a large gain.

We remark on comparable frequencies, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3, for which the commutator argument does not produce a gain anymore. In this case we can prove more favorable estimates relying on novel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates; see Section 3. The interpolation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates with short-time bilinear estimates is referred to as nonlinear interpolation argument and outlined in Section 5.1.

Lastly, we note that for differences of solutions, we have less symmetries at disposal and the integration by parts and commutator arguments can no longer assign the derivative to the lowest frequency. Here the estimate at negative regularities comes to rescue. We show the short-time energy estimates in Section 7.

Linear Strichartz Estimates via Decoupling

In this section we show sharp Strichartz estimates via \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling. To explain the key ingredients, we start with more general considerations, relating Knapp examples with the notion of flat sets.

Decoupling into flat sets and the Knapp example

Decoupling estimates origin in the work of Wolff [60] on local smoothing for the Euclidean wave equation; see also [15, 42]. Finally, Bourgain–Demeter [9] obtained sharp \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling estimates for elliptic surfaces and their conical extensions. Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{\Delta } f(x,t) = \int _{|\xi | \leqq 1} e^{i(\langle x, \xi \rangle + t |\xi |^2)} f(\xi ) \textrm{d}\xi \end{aligned}$$\end{document}EΔf(x,t)=∫|ξ|≦1ei(⟨x,ξ⟩+t|ξ|2)f(ξ)dξdenote the Fourier extension operator for the paraboloid. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling estimates read\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {E}_{\Delta } f \Vert _{L^p_{t,x}(B_{d+1}(0,R))} \lesssim _\varepsilon R^\varepsilon \big ( \sum _{\theta :R^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\text {ball}} \Vert \mathcal {E}_{\Delta } f_{\theta } \Vert ^2_{L^p_{t,x}(w_{B_{d+1}(0,R)})} \big )^{\frac{1}{2}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖EΔf‖Lt,xp(Bd+1(0,R))≲εRε(∑θ:R-12-ball‖EΔfθ‖Lt,xp(wBd+1(0,R))2)12for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \leqq p \leqq \frac{2(d+2)}{d}$$\end{document}2≦p≦2(d+2)d. Above \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w_{B_{d+1}}(0,R)$$\end{document}wBd+1(0,R) denotes a weight with high polynomial decay away from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B_{d+1}(0,R)$$\end{document}Bd+1(0,R). The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$R^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}R-12-balls are distinguished for elliptic surfaces as on scales \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$|(x,t)| \leqq R$$\end{document}|(x,t)|≦R these trivialize the Fourier extension. This is a simple consequence of Taylor expanding the phase function. By approximating exponential sums with the Fourier extension operator, Bourgain–Demeter [9] proved sharp (up to endpoints) Strichartz estimates for elliptic Schrödinger equations on rational and irrational tori.

Whereas the Strichartz estimates for elliptic Schrödinger equations on tori for finite times resemble the estimates on Euclidean space up to arbitrarily small additional derivative loss, this drastically fails for hyperbolic Schrödinger equations (see also [10]). We shall see below that the fKP-II surfaces are non-elliptic.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Box = \partial _1^2 - \partial _2^2$$\end{document}□=∂12-∂22 denote the hyperbolic Laplacian in two dimensions. Then, due to the dispersive estimate, on Euclidean space it holds\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert e^{it \Box } u_0 \Vert _{L_t^4(\mathbb {R};L^4_{xy}(\mathbb {R}^2))} \lesssim \Vert u_0 \Vert _{L^2_{xy}(\mathbb {R}^2)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖eit□u0‖Lt4(R;Lxy4(R2))≲‖u0‖Lxy2(R2).On the square torus there is no oscillation on the diagonal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{ \xi = \eta \}$$\end{document}{ξ=η}, which points out that the following \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimate is sharp:10\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N e^{it \Box } u_0 \Vert _{L_t^4([0,1],L^4_{xy}(\mathbb {T}^2))} \lesssim N^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert u_0 \Vert _{L^2(\mathbb {T}^2)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNeit□u0‖Lt4([0,1],Lxy4(T2))≲N14‖u0‖L2(T2).This has been shown in [16], see also [59] and [55]. But on irrational tori \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}_{\gamma }^2 = \mathbb {T}\times \gamma \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}Tγ2=T×γT, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \in (1/2,1] \backslash \mathbb {Q}$$\end{document}γ∈(1/2,1]\Q, this example is clearly excluded, which leads to the natural question of a possible improvement of (10) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {T}^2_\gamma $$\end{document}Tγ2. This question was recently answered affirmatively by Guth–Maldague–Oh [21], who showed an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling inequality into “flat sets". To describe their result, let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi : \mathbb {R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}φ:R2→R denote a smooth function and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S=\{ ( \xi ,\eta ,\varphi (\xi ,\eta )): |(\xi ,\eta )| \leqq 1 \}$$\end{document}S={(ξ,η,φ(ξ,η)):|(ξ,η)|≦1} be the graph surface. Let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_S f(x,y,t) = \int _{|(\xi ,\eta )| \leqq 1} e^{i(x \xi + y \eta + t \varphi (\xi ,\eta ))} f(\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\xi \textrm{d}\eta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ESf(x,y,t)=∫|(ξ,η)|≦1ei(xξ+yη+tφ(ξ,η))f(ξ,η)dξdηdenote the corresponding Fourier extension operator.

We make the following definition:

Definition 3.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi : \mathbb {R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϕ:R2→R be smooth and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta > 0$$\end{document}δ>0. We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \subseteq [0,1]^2$$\end{document}S⊆[0,1]2 is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\phi ,\delta )$$\end{document}(ϕ,δ)-flat if\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{u,v \in S} | \phi (u) - \phi (v) - \nabla \phi (v) (u-v)| \leqq \delta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supu,v∈S|ϕ(u)-ϕ(v)-∇ϕ(v)(u-v)|≦δ.

Clearly, on flat sets there are no oscillations in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {E}_S$$\end{document}ES. Now, choosing a function which exhausts Bernstein’s inequality, we find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} Recently, Guth–Maldague–Oh [21] proved the following \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell ^2$$\end{document}ℓ2-decoupling result for smooth hypersurfaces in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}^3$$\end{document}R3. We formulate the result for functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f \in \mathcal {S}(\mathbb {R}^3)$$\end{document}f∈S(R3) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp} (\hat{f}) \subseteq \mathcal {N}_{\delta }(\mathcal {M}_\phi )$$\end{document}supp(f^)⊆Nδ(Mϕ). Then, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \subseteq \mathbb {R}^2$$\end{document}S⊆R2 a rectangle, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_S$$\end{document}fS denotes a smoothed Fourier projection to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \times \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}S×R. We have the following result:

Theorem 3.2

( [21, Theorem 1.2]) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi : \mathbb {R}^2 \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϕ:R2→R be a smooth function. for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta > 0$$\end{document}δ>0 there exists a collection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{S \in \mathcal {S}_\delta } \chi _S \leqq C_{\varepsilon ,\phi } \log (\delta ^{-1}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑S∈SδχS≦Cε,ϕlog(δ-1),S is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} the overlapping number is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {O}(\log \delta ^{-1})$$\end{document}O(logδ-1) in the sense that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{S \in \mathcal {S}_\delta } \chi _S \leqq C_{\varepsilon ,\phi } \log (\delta ^{-1}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑S∈SδχS≦Cε,ϕlog(δ-1),

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$2 \leqq p \leqq 4$$\end{document}2≦p≦4 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert f \Vert _{L^p} \leqq C_{\varepsilon , \phi } \delta ^{-\varepsilon } \big ( \sum _{S \in \mathcal {S}_\delta } \Vert f_S \Vert _{L^p}^2 \big )^{\frac{1}{2}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖f‖Lp≦Cε,ϕδ-ε(∑S∈Sδ‖fS‖Lp2)12 for any function f with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(\hat{f}) \subseteq \mathcal {N}_\delta (\mathcal {M}_\phi )$$\end{document}supp(f^)⊆Nδ(Mϕ).

To illustrate this approach to Strichartz estimates, we first turn to the simpler case of the hyperbolic Schrödinger equation on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}R×T. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (\xi _1,\xi _2) = \xi _1^2 - \xi _2^2$$\end{document}φ(ξ1,ξ2)=ξ12-ξ22.

We have the following Strichartz estimate, which is sharp up to endpoints:

Theorem 3.3

The following estimate holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>0$$\end{document}s>0:11\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert e^{it (\partial _1^2 -\partial _2^2)} f \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}([0,1];\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \lesssim \Vert f \Vert _{H^s(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖eit(∂12-∂22)f‖Lt,x,y4([0,1];R×T)≲‖f‖Hs(R×T).

The estimate can be proved using elementary counting arguments reminiscent of the arguments in [56]; see [3]. Here we give a different proof by decoupling into flat sets.

Proof of Theorem 3.3 via decoupling into flat sets

For frequencies of size N, after rescaling, we apply parabolic rescaling \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\xi ,\eta ) \rightarrow (\xi ,\eta )/N$$\end{document}(ξ,η)→(ξ,η)/N, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(x,y) \rightarrow N(x,y)$$\end{document}(x,y)→N(x,y), and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t \rightarrow N^2 t$$\end{document}t→N2t. We obtain\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \Vert P_N e^{it (\partial _1^2 - \partial _2^2)} f \Vert ^4_{L^4_{t,x,y}([0,1] \times \mathbb {T}^2)} \\&= N^{-6} \Vert \int _{|\xi | \leqq 1} \sum _{\eta \in \mathbb {Z}/ N} e^{i(x \xi + y \eta + t (\xi ^2 - \eta ^2))} \hat{f}(N \xi , N \eta ) \textrm{d}\xi \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}([0,N^2] \times N^2 \mathbb {T}^2)}^4. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNeit(∂12-∂22)f‖Lt,x,y4([0,1]×T2)4=N-6‖∫|ξ|≦1∑η∈Z/Nei(xξ+yη+t(ξ2-η2))f^(Nξ,Nη)dξ‖Lt,x,y4([0,N2]×N2T2)4.Now we change to continuous approximation to be in the position to apply the decoupling result:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big \Vert \int _{|\xi | \leqq 1} \sum _{\eta \in \mathbb {Z}/ N} e^{i(x \xi + y \eta + t(\xi ^2 - \eta ^2))} \hat{f}(N \xi , N\eta ) \big \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}(B_{2+1}(0,N^2))} \lesssim \big \Vert \mathcal {E}_{\Box } \tilde{f} \big \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}(w_{2+1}(0,N^2))} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∫|ξ|≦1∑η∈Z/Nei(xξ+yη+t(ξ2-η2))f^(Nξ,Nη)‖Lt,x,y4(B2+1(0,N2))≲‖E□f~‖Lt,x,y4(w2+1(0,N2))with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {E}_{\Box } \tilde{f} = \int _{|(\xi ,\eta )| \leqq 1} e^{i(x \xi + y \eta + t (\xi ^2 - \eta ^2))} \tilde{f}(\xi ,\eta ) \textrm{d}\xi \textrm{d}\eta . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E□f~=∫|(ξ,η)|≦1ei(xξ+yη+t(ξ2-η2))f~(ξ,η)dξdη.Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta = N^{-2}$$\end{document}δ=N-2. Invoking Theorem 3.2 (see also [21, Theorem 2.2] for the construction of the set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta _{\delta }$$\end{document}Θδ) we find that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {E}_{\Box } \tilde{f} \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}(w_{B_{2+1}}(0,\delta ^{-1}))} \lesssim _{\varepsilon } \delta ^{-\varepsilon } \big ( \sum _{\theta \in \Theta _{\delta }} \Vert \mathcal {E}_{\Box } \tilde{f}_{\theta } \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}(w_{B_{2+1}(0,\delta ^{-1})})}^2 \big )^{\frac{1}{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖E□f~‖Lt,x,y4(wB2+1(0,δ-1))≲εδ-ε(∑θ∈Θδ‖E□f~θ‖Lt,x,y4(wB2+1(0,δ-1))2)12,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta $$\end{document}θ being \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta $$\end{document}δ-flat rectangles of dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _1 \times \ell _2$$\end{document}ℓ1×ℓ2 in the null directions of the hyperboloid. 1 \end{pmatrix} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}n1=1211,n2=121-1as for the Hessian of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi (\xi ,\eta ) = \xi ^2 -
