Trilinear Convolution Estimates

The purpose of this section is to obtain trilinear convolution estimates27\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \textrm{d}\xi (\textrm{d}\eta )_1 \textrm{d}\tau \leqq C(N_1,N_2,N_3) \prod _{i=1}^3 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}}\nonumber \\&\quad (1+L_i / N_{i,+}^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{4}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3dξ(dη)1dτ≦C(N1,N2,N3)∏i=13Li12(1+Li/Ni,+α+1)14‖fi,Ni,Li‖2,with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(f_{i,N_i,L_i}) \subseteq D_{\alpha ,N_i,\leqq L_i}$$\end{document}supp(fi,Ni,Li)⊆Dα,Ni,≦Li, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha < 2$$\end{document}α<2. This corresponds to product estimates for solutions and differences of solutions, localized in x-frequency and in time. In the following we suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gtrsim N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N3≳N2. Corresponding to a time localization adjusted to the high frequencies we suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_1,L_3 \gtrsim N_{\max ,+}^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }$$\end{document}L1,L3≳Nmax,+2-α+ε. The above expression will be summable to the short-time norm following Remark 2.6.

For the low frequency more care is required. Since we aim to use the low modulation weight, we suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_2 \gtrsim N_{\max ,+}^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon } \wedge N_{\min ,+}^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}L2≳Nmax,+2-α+ε∧Nmin,+α+1.

The analysis splits into several cases: Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→Low-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\max } \lesssim 1$$\end{document}Nmax≲1. This case is readily estimated by using a Strichartz estimate based on second order transversality.High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3 \gg 1$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3≫1. In the resonant case we can apply the improved \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates, and so do we for some non-resonant cases. When the resonance function becomes large enough, we can conclude favorable estimates using a bilinear Strichartz estimate.High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N3≫N2.

Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→Low-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\max } \lesssim 1$$\end{document}Nmax≲1. This case is readily estimated by using a Strichartz estimate based on second order transversality.

Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→Low-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\max } \lesssim 1$$\end{document}Nmax≲1. This case is readily estimated by using a Strichartz estimate based on second order transversality.

High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3 \gg 1$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3≫1. In the resonant case we can apply the improved \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates, and so do we for some non-resonant cases. When the resonance function becomes large enough, we can conclude favorable estimates using a bilinear Strichartz estimate.

High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3 \gg 1$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3≫1. In the resonant case we can apply the improved \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates, and so do we for some non-resonant cases. When the resonance function becomes large enough, we can conclude favorable estimates using a bilinear Strichartz estimate.

High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N3≫N2.

High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N3≫N2.

Overview of the nonlinear interpolation argument

To obtain (27), we take advantage of the high modulation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max }$$\end{document}Lmax and the corresponding weight \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max }^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}Lmax-12 by estimating the function with high modulation in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2.

One of the key cases to understand is the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction in the resonant case. We shall see that the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates yield estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 up to the critical \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = 4/3$$\end{document}α=4/3. This means that this estimate is very favorable. We perturb the argument to still obtain favorable estimates in the non-resonant case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1^{\alpha } N_2 \ll L_{\max } \lesssim M^*$$\end{document}N1αN2≪Lmax≲M∗ and in the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1^{1-\gamma } \lesssim N_2 \lesssim N_1$$\end{document}N11-γ≲N2≲N1. In case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \ll N_1^{1-\gamma }$$\end{document}N2≪N11-γ or \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg M^*$$\end{document}Lmax≫M∗, the short-time bilinear Strichartz estimates are sufficient.

We turn to the estimate of the resonant case in the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction. By symmetry we can assume that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_3 \sim L_{\max }$$\end{document}L3∼Lmax. In the resonant case, note that the resonance relation gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{| \eta _1 \xi _2 - \eta _2 \xi _1 |^2}{N^3} \lesssim L_{\max } \sim N^{\alpha +1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ2-η2ξ1|2N3≲Lmax∼Nα+1.Consequently,28\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≲Nα2.In essence, this yields after an almost orthogonal decomposition a localization\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _i}{\xi _i} - A \big | \lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ηiξi-A|≲Nα2.This allows us to use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates proved in Proposition 3.9, which read as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert S_{\alpha }(t) f \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}([0,1]; \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})} \lesssim _\varepsilon N^{\frac{2-\alpha }{8} + \varepsilon } \Vert f \Vert _{L^2} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Sα(t)f‖Lt,x,y4([0,1];R×T)≲εN2-α8+ε‖f‖L2for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {supp}(\hat{f}) \subseteq \{ (\xi ,\eta ) \in \mathbb {R}^2: |\xi | \sim N, \, \big | \frac{\eta }{\xi } - A \big | \lesssim N^{\frac{\alpha }{2}} \}$$\end{document}supp(f^)⊆{(ξ,η)∈R2:|ξ|∼N,|ηξ-A|≲Nα2}.

By Hölder’s inequality and an application of the transfer principle we find that29\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \textrm{d}\xi (\textrm{d} \eta )_1 \textrm{d}\tau&\leqq \Vert f_{3,N_3,L_3} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \prod _{i=1}^2 \Vert \mathcal {F}_{t,x,y}^{-1} [f_{i,N_i,L_i}] \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}} \\&\lesssim N^{-\frac{\alpha +1}{2}} (N^{\frac{2-\alpha }{8}+\varepsilon })^2 \prod _{i=1}^2 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2, \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3dξ(dη)1dτ≦‖f3,N3,L3‖Lτ,ξ,η2∏i=12‖Ft,x,y-1[fi,Ni,Li]‖Lt,x,y4≲N-α+12(N2-α8+ε)2∏i=12Li12‖fi,Ni,Li‖2,which indeed gives a short-time nonlinear estimate in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > \frac{4}{3}$$\end{document}α>43.

In the non-resonant case, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gg N^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}Lmax≫Nα+1 (28) becomes\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \lesssim \big ( \frac{L_{\max }}{N} \big )^{\frac{1}{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≲(LmaxN)12.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max }$$\end{document}Lmax sufficiently close to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}Nα+1, we can follow along the lines of (29) and apply the Strichartz estimates from Proposition 3.12 instead of Proposition 3.9. But note that applying Proposition 3.12 becomes less and less favorable for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max }$$\end{document}Lmax becoming larger and larger. We impose a threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \ll M^*$$\end{document}Lmax≪M∗, which still gives a favorable estimate.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gtrsim M^*$$\end{document}Lmax≳M∗, we instead use a bilinear Strichartz estimate provided by Lemma 4.1, noting that we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \gtrsim N_{\max }^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≳Nmaxα2.This gives\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1}* f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \textrm{d}\xi (\textrm{d}\eta )_1 \textrm{d}\tau \\&\lesssim (M^*)^{-\frac{1}{2}} (L^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{3,N_3,L_3} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \Vert 1_{D_{N_3,L_3}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim (M^*)^{-\frac{1}{2}} L^{\frac{1}{2}} N^{\frac{1}{2}} L_{12,\min }^{\frac{1}{2}} \langle L_{12,\max } / N^{\frac{\alpha }{2}} \rangle ^{\frac{1}{2}} \prod _{i=1}^3 \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3dξ(dη)1dτ≲(M∗)-12(L12‖f3,N3,L3‖Lτ,ξ,η2‖1DN3,L3(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(M∗)-12L12N12L12,min12⟨L12,max/Nα2⟩12∏i=13‖fi,Ni,Li‖2.This outlines the key interpolation argument for the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction.

We turn to the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction. In case there is a frequency significantly lower than the remaining frequencies, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \ll N_1 \sim N_3$$\end{document}N2≪N1∼N3, we need to distinguish between the high frequency being at high modulation or the low frequency being at high modulation.

First, we consider the case of a high frequency being at high modulation, say \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_3 = L_{\max }$$\end{document}L3=Lmax. In the resonant case we have\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \lesssim N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≲N1α2.Then, we can estimate (27) by Hölder’s inequality and a refined bilinear Strichartz estimate provided by Proposition 4.3:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \textrm{d}\xi (\textrm{d}\eta )_1 \textrm{d}\tau \\&\leqq \Vert f_{3,N_3,L_3} \Vert _{L^2} \Vert 1_{D_{N,L}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim (N_1^{\alpha } N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \log (N_1) L_3^{\frac{1}{2}} N_2^{\frac{1}{2}} L_{12,\min }^{\frac{1}{2}} \langle L_{12,\max } / N_1^{\alpha /2} \rangle ^{\frac{1}{2}} \prod _{i=1}^3 \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _{L^2}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3dξ(dη)1dτ≦‖f3,N3,L3‖L2‖1DN,L(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(N1αN2)-12log(N1)L312N212L12,min12⟨L12,max/N1α/2⟩12∏i=13‖fi,Ni,Li‖L2.By the minimum modulation localization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i \gtrsim N_i^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Li≳Ni2-α+ε for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,3$$\end{document}i=1,3, that is to say our choice of the frequency-dependent time localization, we can continue the above as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lesssim N_1^{-1-\frac{\varepsilon }{2}} \prod _{i=1}^3 L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}≲N1-1-ε2∏i=13Li12‖fi,Ni,Li‖2.The short-time bilinear estimate becomes less favorable for the nonlinear analysis as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \rightarrow N_1$$\end{document}N2→N1. The key point to show local well-posedness as low as in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 is to interpolate with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-estimates when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1^{1-\gamma } \lesssim N_2 \lesssim N_1$$\end{document}N11-γ≲N2≲N1 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ small enough. In the present implementation we will use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = \frac{1}{8}$$\end{document}γ=18 to simplify some of the resulting expressions. It might well be the case that the local well-posedness result can be refined for a different choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ.

Indeed, we find in the resonant case\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \lesssim N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≲N1α2,which implies that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{1,N_1,L_1}]$$\end{document}Ft,x,y-1[f1,N1,L1] can be estimated with Proposition 3.9 after additional almost orthogonal decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _i}{\xi _i} - A \big | \lesssim N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ηiξi-A|≲N1α2.For the estimate of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{2,N_2,L_2}]$$\end{document}Ft,x,y-1[f2,N2,L2] note the following: an application of Proposition 3.12 is not too lossy since we have for the resonance gain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(N_1^{\alpha } N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \ll N_1^{-1}$$\end{document}(N1αN2)-12≪N1-1 in case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow 2$$\end{document}α→2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}γ→0.

In the non-resonant case, we intend to follow along the above lines, where\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _2}{\xi _2} \big | \lesssim \big ( \frac{L_{\max }}{N_2} \big )^{\frac{1}{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η2ξ2|≲(LmaxN2)12.This requires to estimate \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{i,N_i,L_i}]$$\end{document}Ft,x,y-1[fi,Ni,Li] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2 with the lossier estimate provided by Proposition 3.12. Still the resonance gain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max }^{-\frac{1}{2}}$$\end{document}Lmax-12 outweighs the loss from the linear Strichartz estimates imposing a threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \lesssim M^*$$\end{document}Lmax≲M∗. In case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gtrsim M^*$$\end{document}Lmax≳M∗ we can instead use a bilinear Strichartz estimate provided by Lemma 4.1 to find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2})&f_{3,N_3,L_3} \leqq \Vert f_{3,N_3,L_3} \Vert _{L^2} \Vert 1_{D_{N,L}} (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \\&\lesssim (M^*)^{-\frac{1}{2}} L_3^{\frac{1}{2}} N_2^{\frac{1}{2}} L_{12,\min }^{\frac{1}{2}} \langle L_{12,\max } / N_1^{\alpha /2} \rangle ^{\frac{1}{2}} \prod _{i=1}^3 \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3≦‖f3,N3,L3‖L2‖1DN,L(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)‖Lτ,ξ,η2≲(M∗)-12L312N212L12,min12⟨L12,max/N1α/2⟩12∏i=13‖fi,Ni,Li‖2.We will choose \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^* = N_1^{\frac{9}{4}} N_2$$\end{document}M∗=N194N2 to obtain favorable estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (\alpha _0,2)$$\end{document}α∈(α0,2).

It remains to shed light on the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \ll N_1 \sim N_3$$\end{document}N2≪N1∼N3 with the low frequency carrying the high modulation. For the sake of exposition we suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \gtrsim 1$$\end{document}N2≳1. The case of very low frequencies requires possibly a different, but simpler argument.

We would like to use a similar interpolation argument between linear and bilinear Strichartz estimates. A notable change is that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _1}{\xi _1} - \frac{\eta _3}{\xi _3} \big | \lesssim \big ( \frac{L_{\max }}{N_1} \big )^{\frac{1}{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|η1ξ1-η3ξ3|≲(LmaxN1)12.Consequently, in the resonant case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim N_1^{\alpha } N_2$$\end{document}Lmax∼N1αN2, we always have after almost orthogonal decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \big | \frac{\eta _i}{\xi _i} - A \big | \ll N_1^{\frac{\alpha }{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}|ηiξi-A|≪N1α2.This enables us to apply the almost sharp linear Strichartz estimates from Proposition 3.9 to estimate\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y} [f_{i,N_i,L_i}] \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}} \lesssim _\varepsilon N_i^{\frac{2-\alpha }{8}+\varepsilon } L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖Ft,x,y-1[fi,Ni,Li]‖Lt,x,y4≲εNi2-α8+εLi12‖fi,Ni,Li‖2for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,3$$\end{document}i=1,3. We obtain taking into account the modulation weight\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \int (f_{1,N_1,L_1} * f_{2,N_2,L_2}) f_{3,N_3,L_3} \textrm{d}\xi (\textrm{d}\eta )_1 \textrm{d}\tau \\&\leqq \Vert f_{2,N_2,L_2} \Vert _{L^2_{\tau ,\xi ,\eta }} \prod _{i=1,3} \Vert \mathcal {F}^{-1}_{t,x,y}[f_{i,N_i,L_i}] \Vert _{L^4_{t,x,y}} \\&\leqq (N_1^{\alpha } N_2)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \big ( \frac{N_2}{N_1} \big )^{\alpha +1} (N_1^{\frac{2-\alpha }{8}+\varepsilon })^2 \prod _{i=1,3} L_i^{\frac{1}{2}} \Vert f_{i,N_i,L_i} \Vert _2. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫(f1,N1,L1∗f2,N2,L2)f3,N3,L3dξ(dη)1dτ≦‖f2,N2,L2‖Lτ,ξ,η2∏i=1,3‖Ft,x,y-1[fi,Ni,Li]‖Lt,x,y4≦(N1αN2)-12(N2N1)α+1(N12-α8+ε)2∏i=1,3Li12‖fi,Ni,Li‖2.Consequently, although the transversality between the high frequencies is decreased, which leads to potentially worse bilinear Strichartz estimates, the sharp \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^4$$\end{document}L4-Strichartz estimates together with the modulation weight on the low frequency come to rescue.

In the non-resonant case, we can choose a different threshold \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \ll M^{**}$$\end{document}Lmax≪M∗∗, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{**} \gg M^*$$\end{document}M∗∗≫M∗ for which the linear Strichartz estimates still gives us the sharp estimate. With \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{**}$$\end{document}M∗∗ being sufficiently high, the remaining case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \gtrsim M^{**}$$\end{document}Lmax≳M∗∗ can be estimated via a simple bilinear Strichartz estimate from Lemma 4.2. We turn to the implementation.

Proofs of the trilinear convolution estimates

Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→Low-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\max } \lesssim 1$$\end{document}Nmax≲1. In this case we obtain a sufficient estimate by invoking Lemma 4.2.

High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3 \gg 1$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3≫1.

Proposition 5.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1,2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N_3 \gg 1$$\end{document}N1∼N2∼N3≫1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i \gtrsim N_{\max }^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Li≳Nmax2-α+ε. Then the estimate (27) holds with30\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C(N) = N_1^{-\frac{2}{14}-\frac{9\alpha }{14}+\varepsilon }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C(N)=N1-214-9α14+ε.

Proof

Strongly non-resonant case: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim L_{\text {med}} \gtrsim N_1^{\alpha +1}$$\end{document}Lmax∼Lmed≳N1α+1. Suppose that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{\max } \sim L_3 \sim L_2$$\end{document}Lmax∼L3∼L2 by symmetry. High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×Low\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction: Next, we consider the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2$$\end{document}N1∼N3≫N2. Here we distinguish between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \lesssim N_1^{1-\gamma }$$\end{document}N2≲N11-γ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \gtrsim N_1^{1-\gamma }$$\end{document}N2≳N11-γ.

Proposition 5.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (\frac{7}{4},2)$$\end{document}α∈(74,2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1 \ll N_1 \sim N_3 \gg N_2$$\end{document}1≪N1∼N3≫N2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \gtrsim N_1^{-2}$$\end{document}N2≳N1-2, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_i \gtrsim N_{\max }^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }$$\end{document}Li≳Nmax2-α+ε for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,3$$\end{document}i=1,3 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_2 \gtrsim N_{\max }^{2-\alpha +\varepsilon } \wedge N^{\alpha +1}_{\min ,+}$$\end{document}L2≳Nmax2-α+ε∧Nmin,+α+1.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \lesssim N_1^{1-\gamma }$$\end{document}N2≲N11-γ for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<\gamma <1$$\end{document}0<γ<1. Then the estimate (27) holds with34\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_1(N) = N_1^{-1-\frac{\varepsilon }{2}} \vee N_1^{-\frac{3 \alpha }{4}+ \frac{2-\alpha }{4}+ 2 \varepsilon } N_2^{\frac{\alpha }{4}-\frac{1}{2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C1(N)=N1-1-ε2∨N1-3α4+2-α4+2εN2α4-12.For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_1^{1-\gamma } \lesssim N_2 \lesssim N_1$$\end{document}N11-γ≲N2≲N1 we find with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^* \gg N_1^{\alpha } N_2$$\end{document}M∗≫N1αN2 the estimate (27) to hold with:35\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} C_2(N)&= N_2^{-\frac{\alpha +1}{2}} (M^* / N_2^{\alpha +1})^{\frac{1}{24}} (N_1 N_2)^{\frac{1}{8}+\frac{2-\alpha }{12}+\varepsilon } (M^* / (N_2 N_1^{\alpha }) )^{\frac{1}{24}} \\&\quad \quad \vee N_2^{\frac{1}{2}} N_1^{-\frac{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }{2}} (M^*)^{-\frac{1}{2}}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C2(N)=N2-α+12(M∗/N2α+1)124(N1N2)18+2-α12+ε(M∗/(N2N1α))124∨N212N1-2-α+ε2(M∗)-12.

Remark 5.3

We note that in the relevant range \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (7/4,2)$$\end{document}α∈(7/4,2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \gtrsim N_1^{-2}$$\end{document}N2≳N1-2 the first value in (34) is dominating such that we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_1(N) = N_1^{-1-\frac{\varepsilon }{2}}$$\end{document}C1(N)=N1-1-ε2.

We shall frequently apply (35) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = \frac{1}{8}$$\end{document}γ=18 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^* = N_1^{\frac{9}{4}} N_2$$\end{document}M∗=N194N2: The constants become\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{21}(N) = N_1^{\frac{7}{16}-\frac{\alpha }{6}+\varepsilon } N_2^{-\frac{2 \alpha }{3}-\frac{1}{4} +\varepsilon }, \quad C_{22}(N) = N_1^{-\frac{2-\alpha +\varepsilon }{2}} N_1^{-\frac{9}{8}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}C21(N)=N1716-α6+εN2-2α3-14+ε,C22(N)=N1-2-α+ε2N1-98.

Proof

Case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_2 \lesssim N_1^{1-\gamma }$$\end{document}N2≲N11-γ. Short-Time Bilinear Estimates

The purpose of this section is to show short-time bilinear estimates. We show the following:

Proposition 6.1

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0 the following estimate holds:41\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \partial _x (uv) \Vert _{\mathcal {N}^s(T)} \lesssim T^\delta (\Vert u \Vert _{F^s(T)} \Vert v \Vert _{F^0(T)} + \Vert u \Vert _{F^0(T)} \Vert v \Vert _{F^s(T)}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∂x(uv)‖Ns(T)≲Tδ(‖u‖Fs(T)‖v‖F0(T)+‖u‖F0(T)‖v‖Fs(T))provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha > 1.94$$\end{document}α>1.94.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s'= 8(\alpha -2)$$\end{document}s′=8(α-2). The following estimate holds42\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \partial _x(uv) \Vert _{\mathcal {\bar{N}}^{s'}(T)} \lesssim T^\delta \Vert u \Vert _{F^0(T)} \Vert v \Vert _{F^{s'}(T)} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∂x(uv)‖N¯′(T)≲Tδ‖u‖F0(T)‖v‖F′(T)provided that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1.985,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1.985,2).

Proof

We begin with the proof of (41).

First, we reduce the estimates to convolution estimates for functions localized in frequency and modulation. Energy Estimates

In this section we propagate the short-time energy norms of solutions and their differences. In addition to (1) we need to consider the regularized equation when we construct solutions. To this end let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} we set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \widehat{(\tilde{P}_M f)} (\xi ,\eta ) = \varphi _M(\xi ,\eta ) \hat{f}(\xi ,\eta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(P~Mf)^(ξ,η)=φM(ξ,η)f^(ξ,η).Note that the first factor in the definition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} total frequencies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} The Fourier-truncated equation reads59\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \partial _t u - \partial _x D_x^{\alpha } u + \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y^2 u & = \tilde{P}_M (\partial _x (\tilde{P}_M u)^2), \quad (t,x,y) \in \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}, \\ u(0) & = \phi \in E^s. \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-∂xDxαu+∂x-1∂y2u=P~M(∂x(P~Mu)2),(t,x,y)∈R×R×T,u(0)=ϕ∈Es.We formally recover (1) by letting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M = \infty $$\end{document}M=∞.

The estimates for strong solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in F^s(T)$$\end{document}u∈Fs(T) to (59) read:60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert u \Vert ^2_{E^s(T)} \lesssim \Vert u_0 \Vert ^2_{H^{s,0}} + T^\delta \Vert u \Vert ^2_{F^s(T)} \Vert u \Vert _{F^0(T)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖u‖Es(T)2≲‖u0‖s,02+Tδ‖u‖Fs(T)2‖u‖F0(T).In the above estimate we take advantage of the conservative derivative nonlinearity and real-valuedness to place the derivative on the lowest frequency after Littlewood-Paley decomposition.

Secondly, we show estimates for differences of solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v = u_1-u_2$$\end{document}v=u1-u2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i$$\end{document}ui solving (59) at negative Sobolev regularity \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s' = -8(2-\alpha )$$\end{document}s′=-8(2-α):61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert ^2_{E^{s'}(T)} \lesssim \Vert v(0) \Vert ^2_{H^{s',0}} + T^{\delta } \Vert v \Vert ^2_{F^{s'}(T)} (\Vert u_1 \Vert _{F^s(T)} + \Vert u_2 \Vert _{F^s(T)}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖E′(T)2≲‖v(0)‖s′,02+Tδ‖v‖F′(T)2(‖u1‖Fs(T)+‖u2‖Fs(T))for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0.

Energy estimates for solutions

For solutions we show the following:

Proposition 7.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in (0,1]$$\end{document}T∈(0,1], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0} \cup \{ \infty \}$$\end{document}M∈2N0∪{∞}. Let u be a strong solution to (59) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in F^s(T)$$\end{document}u∈Fs(T). Then the estimate (60) holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1.89,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1.89,2).

Proof

The following reductions are standard and we shall be brief. We have by the definition of the function spaces:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_{\leqq 8} u \Vert _{E^s(T)} \lesssim \Vert u_0 \Vert _{H^{s,0}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖P≦8u‖Es(T)≲‖u0‖Hs,0.For high frequencies we find for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0<t\leqq T$$\end{document}0<t≦T by the fundamental theorem of calculus:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N u(t) \Vert _{L^2}^2 = \Vert P_N u(0) \Vert ^2_{L^2} + 2 \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N \tilde{P}_M u \partial _x P_N(\tilde{P}_M u \cdot \tilde{P}_M u) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNu(t)‖L22=‖PNu(0)‖22+2∫0t∫R×TPNP~Mu∂xPN(P~Mu·P~Mu)dxds.In the following we focus on estimating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert P_N u(t) \Vert _{L^2}^2$$\end{document}‖PNu(t)‖L22 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t> 0 $$\end{document}t>0; the estimate for negative times is obtained by symmetric arguments.

Following Remark 2.7 we have for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in F^s(T)$$\end{document}u∈Fs(T) that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert P_N u\Vert ^2_2 \in C^1(0,T)$$\end{document}‖PNu‖22∈C1(0,T). Indeed,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned}&\quad \Vert P_N u(t) \Vert ^2_{L^2} = \Vert S_\alpha (t) P_N u_0 + P_N \int _0^t S_\alpha (t-s) \partial _x \tilde{P}_M ( \tilde{P}_M u(s) \cdot \tilde{P}_M u(s) ) \textrm{d}s \Vert _2^2 \\&= \Vert P_N u_0 \Vert ^2_{L^2} + 2 \langle P_N u_0, \int _0^t S_\alpha (-s) \partial _x \tilde{P}_M (\tilde{P}_M u(s) \tilde{P}_M u(s)) \rangle _{L^2_x} \\&\quad + \langle P_N \int _0^t S_\alpha (-s) \partial _x \tilde{P}_M ( \tilde{P}_M u \cdot \tilde{P}_M u), P_N \int _0^t S_\alpha (-s) \partial _x \tilde{P}_M ( \tilde{P}_M u \cdot \tilde{P}_M u) \rangle _{L^2_x}. \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNu(t)‖22=‖Sα(t)PNu0+PN∫0tSα(t-s)∂xP~M(P~Mu(s)·P~Mu(s))ds‖22=‖PNu0‖22+2⟨PNu0,∫0tSα(-s)∂xP~M(P~Mu(s)P~Mu(s))⟩Lx2+⟨PN∫0tSα(-s)∂xP~M(P~Mu·P~Mu),PN∫0tSα(-s)∂xP~M(P~Mu·P~Mu)⟩Lx2.Moreover,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial _t \int _0^t S_{\alpha }(-s) \partial _x \tilde{P}_M (\tilde{P}_M u(s) \tilde{P}_M u(s)) \textrm{d}s = \partial _x \tilde{P}_M (\tilde{P}_M u(t))^2 \in L^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂t∫0tSα(-s)∂xP~M(P~Mu(s)P~Mu(s))ds=∂xP~M(P~Mu(t))2∈L2.This justifies the application of the fundamental theorem.

We aim to show estimates independent of M. For the sake of brevity we abuse notation and redenote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{P}_M u \rightarrow u$$\end{document}P~Mu→u. To show favorable estimates, we carry out a paraproduct decomposition:62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_N(u^2) = 2 P_N (u P_{\ll N} u) + P_N (P_{\gtrsim N} u P_{\gtrsim N} u). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN(u2)=2PN(uP≪Nu)+PN(P≳NuP≳Nu).We turn to the contribution of the first term: After decomposition\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_N(u P_{\ll N} u) = \sum _{K \ll N} P_N(u P_K u) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN(uP≪Nu)=∑K≪NPN(uPKu)we turn to the estimate of63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sum _{K \ll N} \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N u \partial _x P_N (u P_{K} u) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∑K≪N∫0t∫R×TPNu∂xPN(uPKu)dxds.By a standard commutator argument, we can arrange the derivative on the low frequency. To this end, we write\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_N (u P_{K} u) = P_N u P_K u + [ P_N (u P_{K} u) - P_N u P_K u]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN(uPKu)=PNuPKu+[PN(uPKu)-PNuPKu].For the first term, integration by parts is straight-forward:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N u \partial _x (P_N u P_K u) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}s = \frac{1}{2} \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} (P_N u)^2 \partial _x P_K u \, \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫0t∫R×TPNu∂x(PNuPKu)dxds=12∫0t∫R×T(PNu)2∂xPKudxds.For the second term, we can expand the multiplier in Fourier space to see that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {[} P_N (u P_{K} u) - P_N u P_K u] \sim \frac{K}{N} P_N' u P_K u. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}[PN(uPKu)-PNuPKu]∼KNPN′uPKu.Here \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P'_N = P_{N/2} + P_N + P_{2N}$$\end{document}PN′=PN/2+PN+P2N denotes a mild enlargement of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_N$$\end{document}PN.

We turn to the second term. Expand the second term on the left hand side of (62) as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} P_N (P_{\gtrsim N} u P_{\gtrsim N} u )&= \sum _{N_1 \sim N_2 \gtrsim N} P_N (P_{N_1} u P_{N_2} u) \\&= \sum _{N_1 \sim N_2 \sim N} P_N (P_{N_1} u P_{N_2} u) + \sum _{N_1 \sim N_2 \gg N} P_N (P_{N_1} u P_{N_2} u). \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}PN(P≳NuP≳Nu)=∑N1∼N2≳NPN(PN1uPN2u)=∑N1∼N2∼NPN(PN1uPN2u)+∑N1∼N2≫NPN(PN1uPN2u).The High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction corresponds to the first term in the above display and the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→Low-interaction corresponds to the second term.

We estimate the High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\times $$\end{document}×High\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rightarrow $$\end{document}→High-interaction as follows. Proposition 7.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \in (0,1]$$\end{document}T∈(0,1], \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1.972,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1.972,2), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \in 2^{\mathbb {N}_0} \cup \{ \infty \}$$\end{document}M∈2N0∪{∞} and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_i \in F^s(T)$$\end{document}ui∈Fs(T), \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1,2$$\end{document}i=1,2 solve (59). Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s'=-8(2-\alpha )$$\end{document}s′=-8(2-α). Then (61) holds.

Proof

We follow the same arguments like in the previous subsection. By the fundamental theorem of calculus, using that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert P_{N} v(t) \Vert ^2_{L^2}$$\end{document}‖PNv(t)‖22 is continuously differentiable in t, we find for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \geqq 1$$\end{document}N≧1:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N v(t) \Vert ^2_{L^2} = \Vert P_N v(0) \Vert ^2_{L^2} + 2 \int _0^t \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} P_N (\tilde{P}_M v) \partial _x P_N (\tilde{P}_M v \, \tilde{P}_M(u_1+u_2)) \textrm{d}x \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PNv(t)‖22=‖PNv(0)‖22+2∫0t∫R×TPN(P~Mv)∂xPN(P~MvP~M(u1+u2))dxds.We redenote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{P}_M v \rightarrow v$$\end{document}P~Mv→v and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{P}_M u_i \rightarrow u_i$$\end{document}P~Mui→ui to ease notation. This can be reduced to a previous estimate: We have seen when estimating the first term in (62) that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts}
