Conclusion of Local Well-Posedness with Short-Time Estimates

In [28] we have applied the Ionescu–Kenig–Tataru argument [31] to the KP-II equation in the periodic setting. Since this applies with only minor changes here, we shall be brief. Showing the existence of solutions in an appropriate scale of regularity requires further explanation.

The proof of local well-posedness is carried out in three steps: Establishing the existence of solutions and a priori estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s(T)$$\end{document}Fs(T) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0,Estimates for the solution to the difference equation at negative regularity,Conclusion of continuous dependence via frequency envelopes.We remark that the proof of existence employs a priori estimates for a regularized equation. For this reason we do not separate the construction of solutions and the proof of a priori estimates.

Establishing the existence of solutions and a priori estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s(T)$$\end{document}Fs(T) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0,

Establishing the existence of solutions and a priori estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F^s(T)$$\end{document}Fs(T) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 0$$\end{document}s≧0,

Conclusion of continuous dependence via frequency envelopes.

The frequency-dependent time localization, as sketched in Section 2.3, is given by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T=T(N)=N^{-(2-\alpha +\varepsilon )}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T=T(N)=N-(2-α+ε).The extra time localization \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N^{-\varepsilon }$$\end{document}N-ε will be useful to overcome certain logarithmic divergences.

Existence of solutions and global a priori estimates

We construct solutions to73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \partial _t u - \partial _x D_x^{\alpha } u + \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y^2 u & = u \partial _x u, \quad (t,x,y) \in \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}\\ u(0) & = \phi \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂tu-∂xDxαu+∂x-1∂y2u=u∂xu,(t,x,y)∈R×R×Tu(0)=ϕfor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (1.94,2)$$\end{document}α∈(1.94,2). This is more admissible than stated in Theorem 1.1. Since we are exclusively analyzing solutions, which satisfy more symmetries than differences of solutions, we obtain a larger range.

The construction employs a Galerkin approximation and the Aubin–Lions compactness lemma (cf. [45]). The argument follows to a great extent the construction of solutions to KP-I equations in three dimensions (cf. [27]).

Recall the regularity scale \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^s$$\end{document}Bs introduced in (3). Clearly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^1 \hookrightarrow H^1$$\end{document}B1↪H1 and on bounded domains \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_R = (-R,R) \times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}DR=(-R,R)×T, the embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(D_R) \hookrightarrow L^2(D_R)$$\end{document}H1(DR)↪L2(DR) is compact. This will allow us to construct global solutions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_T L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}CTL2(R×T) by compactness arguments.

We shall see below that this symbol behaves well with commutator arguments and the derivative nonlinearity. This is the main reason for not working directly in isotropic Sobolev spaces. We have the following:

Proposition 8.1

For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 2$$\end{document}s≧2 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T>0$$\end{document}T>0 there is a mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_T^s: B^s \rightarrow L_T^{\infty } B^s$$\end{document}STs:Bs→LT∞Bs with\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S_T^s(\phi ) \in C_T^1 H^{-1} \cap C_T H^1 \cap C_T B^0 \end{aligned}$$\end{document}STs(ϕ)∈CT1H-1∩CTH1∩CTB0and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_T^s (\phi )$$\end{document}STs(ϕ) is the unique distributional solution to (73).

Consequently, there is a data-to-solution mapping \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_T^{\infty }: B^{\infty } \rightarrow C_T B^{\infty }$$\end{document}ST∞:B∞→CTB∞ for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T > 0$$\end{document}T>0. Once the proposition is established, we can work with solutions in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_T B^{\infty }$$\end{document}CTB∞ since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^{\infty } \hookrightarrow L^2(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}B∞↪L2(R×T) is dense. When the continuity in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^{s,0}$$\end{document}Hs,0 is established, we can extend \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_T^s:B^s \rightarrow C([-T,T],B^s)$$\end{document}STs:Bs→C([-T,T],Bs) to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_T^s:H^{s,0} \rightarrow C([-T,T],H^{s,0})$$\end{document}STs:Hs,0→C([-T,T],Hs,0).

Proposition 8.2

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 1$$\end{document}s≧1 the following estimates hold:75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \Vert u^M \Vert _{\bar{F}^s(T)} & \lesssim \Vert u^M \Vert _{\bar{E}^s(T)} + \Vert \tilde{P}_M (\partial _x ( \tilde{P}_M u^M)^2) \Vert _{\bar{\mathcal {N}}^s(T)}, \\ \Vert \partial _x \tilde{P}_M ((\tilde{P}_M u^M)^2) \Vert _{\bar{\mathcal {N}}^s(T)} & \lesssim T^{\delta } \Vert u^M \Vert _{\bar{F}^s(T)} \Vert u^M \Vert _{F^s(T)}, \\ \Vert u^M \Vert ^2_{\bar{E}^s(T)} & \lesssim \Vert \phi \Vert ^2_{B^s} + T^{\delta } \Vert u^M \Vert ^2_{\bar{F}^s(T)} \Vert u^M \Vert _{F^s(T)}. \end{array} \right. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖uM‖F¯s(T)≲‖uM‖E¯s(T)+‖P~M(∂x(P~MuM)2)‖N¯s(T),‖∂xP~M((P~MuM)2)‖N¯s(T)≲Tδ‖uM‖F¯s(T)‖uM‖Fs(T),‖uM‖E¯s(T)2≲‖ϕ‖s2+Tδ‖uM‖F¯s(T)2‖uM‖Fs(T).

The short-time analysis actually yields improved estimates, but these are presently not required.

Proof of Proposition 8.1, ctd: Applying the Aubin-Lions lemma.

The short-time estimates in weighted norms (75) together with the already established global estimates for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Vert u^M \Vert _{F^s(T)}$$\end{document}‖uM‖Fs(T) we find global a priori estimates in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$B^s$$\end{document}Bs for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \geqq 1$$\end{document}s≧1:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \sup _{t \in [-T,T]} \Vert u^M(t) \Vert _{B^s} \lesssim _{T,s} \Vert \phi \Vert _{B^s}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}supt∈[-T,T]‖uM(t)‖Bs≲T,s‖ϕ‖Bs.To summarize, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(u^M)_{M \in 2^{\mathbb {N}}}$$\end{document}(uM)M∈2N exist globally in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_T^{\infty } B^1$$\end{document}LT∞B1 and satisfies bounds (for fixed T) independent of M. For any domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_R = (-R,R) \times \mathbb {T}$$\end{document}DR=(-R,R)×T we have bounds uniform in M and R:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} u^M \in L_T^{\infty } H^1(D_R), \quad \partial _t u^M \in L_T^{\infty } H^{-1} (D_R). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}uM∈LT∞H1(DR),∂tuM∈LT∞H-1(DR).By the compact embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H^1(D_R) \hookrightarrow L^2(D_R)$$\end{document}H1(DR)↪L2(DR) and the continuous embedding \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(D_R) \hookrightarrow H^{-1}(D_R)$$\end{document}L2(DR)↪H-1(DR), we can apply the Aubin–Lions lemma to obtain a subsequence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u^M \rightarrow u \in C_T L^2_{\text {loc}}$$\end{document}uM→u∈CTLloc2. We have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L_T^{\infty } B^s$$\end{document}u∈LT∞Bs, consequently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in L_T^{\infty } H^1$$\end{document}u∈LT∞H1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _x^{-1} \partial _y u \in L_T^{\infty } L^2$$\end{document}∂x-1∂yu∈LT∞L2.

By dual pairing in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi \in C^\infty _c([-T,T],\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})$$\end{document}φ∈Cc∞([-T,T],R×T) and passing to the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M \rightarrow \infty $$\end{document}M→∞, we find that u is a distributional solution to fKP-II. Since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C^1([-T,T],H^{-1}) \cap L_T^{\infty } H^1$$\end{document}u∈C1([-T,T],H-1)∩LT∞H1, we conclude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C([-T,T],H^{\sigma })$$\end{document}u∈C([-T,T],Hσ) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \in [-1,1)$$\end{document}σ∈[-1,1). For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>1$$\end{document}s>1 we have moreover \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C_T H^1$$\end{document}u∈CTH1.

Lastly, we show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C_T B^0$$\end{document}u∈CTB0 for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>1$$\end{document}s>1. We have already established \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u \in C_T L^2$$\end{document}u∈CTL2. Now it suffices to show that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$w = \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y u \in C_T L^2$$\end{document}w=∂x-1∂yu∈CTL2. To this end, we use Duhamel’s formula\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} w(t_2) - w(t_1) = ( S_\alpha (t_2) - S_\alpha (t_1)) \partial _x^{-1} \partial _y \phi - \int _{t_1}^{t_2} S_\alpha (t_2-s) (u (s) \partial _x w(s)) \textrm{d}s. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}w(t2)-w(t1)=(Sα(t2)-Sα(t1))∂x-1∂yϕ-∫t12Sα(t2-s)(u(s)∂xw(s))ds.The linear term converges to zero in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2 as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_1 \rightarrow t_2$$\end{document}t1→t2 because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(S_\alpha (t))_{t \in \mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}(Sα(t))t∈R is a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_0$$\end{document}C0-semigroup. For the second term we estimate with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I = [t_1,t_2]$$\end{document}I=[t1,t2] for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s>1$$\end{document}s>1:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert \int _{t_1}^{t_2} S_\alpha (t_2-s) (u (s) \partial _x w(s)) \textrm{d}s \Vert _{L^2} \leqq \Vert u \Vert _{L_I^1 L^{\infty }_{x,y}} \Vert \partial _x w \Vert _{L^{\infty }_I L^2_{x,y}} \lesssim \Vert u \Vert _{L^1_I B^s} \Vert w \Vert _{L^\infty _I B^1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖∫t12Sα(t2-s)(u(s)∂xw(s))ds‖L2≦‖u‖LI1Lx,y∞‖∂xw‖LI∞Lx,y2≲‖u‖LI1Bs‖w‖LI∞B1.The ultimate estimate follows from Sobolev embedding. Lipschitz continuous dependence at negative Sobolev regularity

In the next step we show Lipschitz continuous dependence in negative Sobolev regularities for solutions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$u_1,u_2$$\end{document}u1,u2 in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2$$\end{document}L2. Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v=u_1-u_2$$\end{document}v=u1-u2 denote the differences of solutions to (73). v solves the following Cauchy problem:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left\{ \begin{array}{cl} \partial _t v - Subsuming the estimates we find that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert ^2_{F^{s'}(T)} \lesssim \Vert v_0 \Vert ^2_{H^{s',0}} + T^\delta \Vert v \Vert ^2_{F^{s'}(T)} ( \Vert u_1 \Vert _{F^0(T)}^2 + \Vert u_2 \Vert _{F^0(T)}^2 + \Vert u_1 \Vert _{F^0(T)} + \Vert u_2 \Vert _{F^0(T)} ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖F′(T)2≲‖v0‖s′,02+Tδ‖v‖F′(T)2(‖u1‖F0(T)2+‖u2‖F0(T)2+‖u1‖F0(T)+‖u2‖F0(T)).By the previous step we find for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \leqq T_0(\Vert u_i(0) \Vert _{L^2})$$\end{document}T≦T0(‖ui(0)‖L2):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert ^2_{F^{s'}(T)} \lesssim \Vert v_0 \Vert _{H^{s',0}}^2 + T^\delta \Vert v \Vert ^2_{F^{s'}(T)} ( \Vert u_1(0) \Vert _{L^2}^2 + \Vert u_2(0) \Vert _{L^2}^2 + \Vert u_1(0) \Vert _{L^2} + \Vert u_2(0) \Vert _{L^2}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖F′(T)2≲‖v0‖Hs′,02+Tδ‖v‖F′(T)2(‖u1(0)‖L22+‖u2(0)‖L22+‖u1(0)‖L2+‖u2(0)‖L2).Choosing T smaller, if necessary, we find\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert v \Vert _{F^{s'}(T)} \lesssim \Vert v_0 \Vert _{H^{s',0}(\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖v‖F′(T)≲‖v0‖Hs′,0(R×T).

Conclusion of continuous dependence via frequency envelopes

At this point the continuous dependence follows from invoking the frequency envelope argument. For details we refer to our previous implementation in [28] to avoid repetition. The proof of Theorem 1.1 is complete.

A Long-Time Property

In this section we prove Theorem 1.2. This elaborates on the proof of a similar result in [47] in the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =2$$\end{document}α=2. Indeed, Theorem 1.2 with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha =2$$\end{document}α=2 is the contained in [47, Lemma 5.3]. In order to deal with the fractional dispersion in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\xi $$\end{document}ξ-frequencies, we will rely on some new arguments, in particular on [34, Lemmas 6, 7]. The proof of [47, Claim 5.1] no longer works in the case of low fractional dispersion. We will use instead an adaptation of the argument used in [5, Lemma 2.1] to the case of periodic solutions with respect to the transverse variable.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$r>1$$\end{document}r>1, which presently will be chosen close to 1, we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \varphi (x)=\int _{-\infty }^x (1+y^2)^{-\frac{r}{2}}\, \textrm{d}y. $$\end{document}φ(x)=∫-∞x(1+y2)-r2dy.The main point is the following lemma:

Lemma 9.1

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (4/3,2]$$\end{document}α∈(4/3,2]. There is a constant C independent of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_0\in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}x0∈R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0 < \varepsilon \leqq 1$$\end{document}0<ε≦1 such that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}}\, (\varphi '(\varepsilon ( x+x_0)))^2\, u^4(x,y)\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}y \\ & \quad \leqq C (\Vert \langle D_x\rangle ^{\alpha /2}((\varphi '(\varepsilon ( x+x_0-ct)))^{\frac{1}{2}}u(x,y )) \Vert ^4_{L^2_{x,y}} + \Vert (\varphi '(\varepsilon ( x+x_0)))^{\frac{1}{2}}\partial _x^{-1}\partial _y u(x,y) \Vert ^4_{L^2_{x,y}}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫R×T(φ′(ε(x+x0)))2u4(x,y)dxdy≦C(‖⟨Dx⟩α/2((φ′(ε(x+x0-ct)))12u(x,y))‖Lx,y24+‖(φ′(ε(x+x0)))12∂x-1∂yu(x,y)‖Lx,y24).

Before we turn to the proof in earnest, we recall the following simple commutator estimate (see for example [4, Lemma 2.97] for a proof):

Lemma 9.2

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P_N$$\end{document}PN denote the Littlewood-Paley projection in the x-variable for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N \in 2^{\mathbb {Z}}$$\end{document}N∈2Z. Proof of Lemma 9.1

In the following we focus on estimating \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta (y) u$$\end{document}θ(y)u, which is redenoted by u for convenience.

Next, we write for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Using an integration by parts in x, we can write that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ II\lesssim \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} |\partial _x \big (\langle D_x\rangle ^{-2\sigma }(\psi _{\varepsilon } u)\psi _{\varepsilon }\big )|\, |\partial _x^{-1}\partial _y u|. $$\end{document}II≲∫R×T|∂x(⟨Dx⟩-2σ(ψεu)ψε)||∂x-1∂yu|.We now fix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ via the relation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1-2\sigma =\alpha /2$$\end{document}1-2σ=α/2, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \sigma =\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\alpha }{4}. $$\end{document}σ=12-α4. Consequently, we obtain the estimate89\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Vert P_N(\psi _{\varepsilon } u)\Vert ^2_{L^2_{x}}\lesssim N^{2\sigma } \big ( \Vert \langle D_x\rangle ^{\alpha /2}(\psi _\varepsilon u) \Vert ^2_{L^2_{x,y}} + \Vert \psi _{\varepsilon } \partial _x^{-1}\partial _y u \Vert ^2_{L^2_{x,y}} \big ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}‖PN(ψεu)‖Lx22≲N2σ(‖⟨Dx⟩α/2(ψεu)‖Lx,y22+‖ψε∂x-1∂yu‖Lx,y22).It now remains to suitably interpolate between (88) and (89). This completes the proof of Lemma 9.1. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

With Lemma 9.1 in hand, we can complete the proof of Theorem 1.2.

Proof of Theorem 1.2

If u is a solution to (1), then90\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{1}{2} \frac{d}{\textrm{d}t} \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}}\, \varphi (\varepsilon (x+x_0-ct)) u^2(t,x,y)\textrm{d}x\textrm{d}y =-\int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} \partial _x(\varphi (\varepsilon (x+x_0-ct)) u)\, D_x^\alpha u \nonumber \\ & \quad -\frac{\varepsilon }{6}\int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}}\varphi '(\varepsilon (x+x_0-ct)) u^3 -\frac{\varepsilon }{2} \int _{\mathbb {R}\times \mathbb {T}} \,\varphi '(\varepsilon (x+x_0-ct))\, \big ((\partial _x^{-1}\partial _y u)^2+cu^2\big )\,.\nonumber \\ \end{aligned}$$\end{document}12ddt∫R×Tφ(ε(x+x0-ct))u2(t,x,y)dxdy=-∫R×T∂x(φ(ε(x+x0-ct))u)Dxαu-ε6∫R×Tφ′(ε(x+x0-ct))u3-ε2∫R×Tφ′(ε(x+x0-ct))((∂x-1∂yu)2+cu2).We now evaluate each term on the right hand-side of (90). This completes the proof of Theorem 1.2.
